Asymptotics of dimer coverings on free boundary rail-yard graphs
Zhongyang Li

Abstract
. Rail-yard graphs are a general class of graphs introduced in [ 8 ] on which the random dimer coverings form Schur processes. We study asymptotic limits of random dimer coverings on rail yard graphs with free boundary conditions on both the left boundary and the right boundary (double-sided free boundary) when the mesh sizes of the graphs go to 0. Each dimer covering corresponds to a sequence of interlacing partitions starting with an arbitrary partition and ending in an arbitrary partition. Under the assumption that the probability of each dimer covering is proportional to the product of weights of present edges, we obtain the moment formula for the height function which includes an infinite product. By passing down to the scaling limit, we compute the limit shape (law of large numbers) of the rescaled height functions and prove the convergence of unrescaled height fluctuations to a diffeomorphic image of the restriction of the 0-boundary Gaussian free field (central limit theorem) on the upper half plane to a subset. Applications include the limit shape and height fluctuations for free boundary steep tilings as proposed in [ 10 ] . The technique to obtain these results is to analyze a class of Macdonald processes with dual specializations, subject to further complexities arising from the infinite product in the moment formula. We also obtain a new algorithm to sample double-sided free boundary dimer coverings on rail-yard graphs, which fulfills an open problem in [ 3 ] .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Backgrounds

A perfect matching, or a dimer covering on a graph is a subset of edges in which each vertex is incident to exactly one edge.
The dimer model, defined as a probability measure on the set of all the perfect matchings (See
[
22
]
), is a natural mathematical model for the structures of matter. For example, the double-bond configurations of a graphite molecule correspond to
perfect matchings on a hexagonal lattice; the 2D Ising model has a measure-preserving correspondence with the dimer model on a Fisher graph (see
[
16
,
34
,
26
]
).

Probability measures on the set of all perfect matchings of a graph are usually defined depending on the energy of the dimer configuration, quantified as the product of weights of present edges in the configuration. Empirical results show that in large graphs, there are certain regions where the configuration is almost deterministic, i.e. only one type of edges have very high probability to occur in the dimer configuration. These are called “frozen regions”, and their boundaries are called “frozen boundaries” or “arctic curves”.
Mathematicians and physicists developed tools to investigate this phenomenon in a rigorous way. A combinatorial argument shows that the total number of perfect matchings on any finite planar graph can be computed by the Pfaffian of the corresponding weighted adjacency matrix (
[
20
,
18
]
). The local statistics can be computed by the inverse of the weighted adjacency matrix (
[
21
]
); a complete picture of phase transitions was obtained in
[
24
]
.
When the mesh size of the graph goes to 0 so that the graph approximates a simply-connected region in the plane, the limit shape of height functions can be obtained by a variational principle (
[
12
]
), and the frozen boundary is proved to be an algebraic curve of a specific type called the cloud curve (
[
23
]
). It is also known that the fluctuations of (unrescaled) dimer heights converge to the Gaussian free field (GFF) in distribution when the boundary satisfies certain conditions (
[
21
,
27
]
).

In this paper, we study perfect matchings on a general class of bipartite graphs called the rail-yard graph, which was first defined in
[
8
]
.
For rail yard graphs, special cases include the Aztec diamond (
[
14
,
15
,
19
,
11
]
), the pyramid partition (
[
39
]
), the steep tiling (
[
10
]
), the tower graph (
[
7
]
), the contracting square-hexagon lattice (
[
9
,
29
,
28
,
25
]
) and the contracting bipartite graph (
[
30
]
).

The major goal of the paper is to study the limit shape and height fluctuations of double-sided free boundary dimer coverings on such graphs. The double-sided free boundary condition is again a general class of boundary conditions.
Asymptotics of dimer coverings on contracting hexagonal lattice with one free boundary and one empty boundary was studied in
[
37
]
. Asymptotics of Schur processes with one free boundary and one empty boundary were studied in
[
4
]
. Asymptotics of dimer coverings on rail yard graph with one specified boundary condition and one empty boundary condition were studied in
[
31
]
.

Unlike
[
37
]
and
[
4
]
, we discuss dimer coverings on rail yard graphs with two free boundaries. When parameters are specifically chosen, this model may degenerate to either one free boundary and one empty boundary or two empty boundaries; the latter is also called pure dimer coverings.
Free boundary dimer coverings on rail-yard graphs are in one-to-one correspondence with sequences of interlacing partitions, whose probability distribution forms a Macdonald process. The observables of Macdonald processes have been extensively studied in
[
5
,
6
]
, and applied to study the asymptotics of lozenge tilings in
[
13
,
2
]
; and pure dimer coverings on rail-yard graphs in
[
32
]
. Unlike
[
13
,
2
]
, the Macdonald processes used to study dimer configurations on rail-yard graph involve dual partitions as well. Such Macdonald processes can also be obtained from the Macdonald processes defined in
[
5
,
6
]
by certain specializations, which are homomorphisms from the algebra of symmetric polynomials to the set of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, but can not be realized as function evaluations. The observables of Macdonald processes studied in this paper was originally discussed in
[
35
]
.

1.2.
Technical innovations and significance of results.

Compared to
[
32
]
, this paper includes both technical innovations and generalizations of results that solving well-known open problems in the following aspects

•

A new moment formula (Lemma
3.8
) is obtained for the height functions of perfect matchings on the doubly free boundary rail-yard graphs. Proving this formula needs a number of new combinatorial results (Lemmas
3.9
-
3.12
).

•

Analyzing the new formula in Lemma
3.8
needs new combinatorial and analytic arguments, including dealing with an infinite product using convergence theorems in complex analysis; see Section
4
.

•

As a result, we obtain the law of large numbers and the central limit theorem for perfect matchings on a large class of graphs, including the well-known free boundary steep tilings in (
[
10
]
); filling a gap in the literature.

•

The best previous known results of free boundary Schur processes are the limit shape results with empty boundary condition on one boundary and free boundary condition on the other boundary; see
[
37
,
4
]
. In this paper we obtain first results about asymptotics of Schur processes with doubly free boundary conditions - not only the limit shape of the rescaled height function, but also the convergence to GFF of unrescaled height function.

•

The limit shape is obtained by analyzing the roots of an infinite product; compared to analyzing the roots of finte products in
[
32
]
, the former requires deep convergence results in complex analysis; see Section
5
.

•

It is worth noting that the GFF we obtained in this paper, as limit of the unrescaled height fluctuations, is not the zero-boundary GFF in the upper half plane, but the restriction of the zero-boundnary GFF in the upper half plane to a subset; see Section
6
. This appears to be the first time to obtain a GFF of this type as the scaling limit of statistical mechanical models.

•

We also obtain a new sampling algorithm to sample doubly-free boundary Schur processes, solving an open problem in
[
3
]
. This algorithm cannot be obtained from the usual Markov chain Monte Carlo approach. Details on this are explained below.

Samplings of typical dimer configurations on a large graph give us empirical understandings of the limit shape. One approach to sample typical dimer configurations is the Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithm based on the fact that any discrete-time, irreducible, aperiodic, finite-state Markov chain converges to its unique stationary distribution as the time goes to infinity. Hence one may construct a Markov chain transition matrix whose state space consists of all the dimer configurations on a large finite graph, and stationary distribution is given by the probability measure in which the probability of each dimer configuration is proportional to the product of weights of edges (Gibbs distribution); a jump in this chain is given by a “shuffling”, or equivalently, moving dimer configurations on a single face of even degree to alternating edges. Then if the chain is aperiodic and irreducible, running the chain for sufficiently many steps will give a typical dimer configuration whose distribution is close to the Gibbs distribution. This approach was used in
[
31
]
to sample perfect matchings on rail-yard graphs with specified boundary conditions.

The MCMC algorithm does not seem to work directly to sample the free-boundary dimer configurations on rail-yard graphs. The major obstacle here is the fact that the Markov chain constructed in this way with sample space consisting of all the free-boundary dimer coverings is no longer irreducible. For Schur processes with free boundary condition on one boundary, and empty boundary condition on the other, a perfect sampling algorithm was given in
[
3
]
, yet the sampling of double-sided free boundary Schur processes was left open in
[
3
]
. In this paper, we develop an algorithm to sample the double-sided free boundary Schur processes. The idea is based on repeatedly commuting and reflecting the Bosonic operators with respect to the
left and right boundary, inspired by the computation of partition functions of double-sided free boundary steep tilings in
[
10
]
. Since the partition function includes an infinite product, the sampling algorithm here to sample the double-sided free boundary Schur processes is not an exact sampling, but the sample obtained by this algorithm has the distribution an approximation of the Gibbs measure, if we run the algorithm for sufficiently many steps.

1.3.
Paper organization.

The organization of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
, we define the rail-yard graph, the perfect matching and the height function, review related technical facts, state assumptions on graph parameters and main results of the paper. In Section
3
, we compute the moments of height functions of perfect matchings on rail-yard graphs by computing the observables in a Macdonald processes with dual specialization. In Section
4
, we study the asymptotics of the moments of the random height functions and prove its Gaussian fluctuation in the scaling limit. In Section
5
, we prove an integral formula for the Laplace transform of the rescaled height function (see Lemma
2.7
), which turns out to be deterministic, as a 2D analog of law of large numbers. We further obtain an the explicit formula for the frozen boundary in the scaling limit. In Section
6
, we prove that the fluctuations of unrescaled height function converges to the pull-back of the restriction Gaussian Free Field (GFF) in the upper half plane to a subset under a diffeomorphism from the liquid region to the subset of the upper half plane (see Theorem
2.10
). In Section
7
, we introduce a new algorithm to sample double-sided free boundary Schur processes. In Section
8
, we discuss a specific example of the rail-yard graph, such that the limit shape and height fluctuations of perfect matchings on the graphs can be obtained by the technique developed in the paper; and random dimer coverings on the graph can be sampled by the algorithm introduced in Section
8
. In Appendix
A
we review facts about Macdonald polynomials and include some known technical results used in this paper.

2.
Backgrounds

In this section, we define the rail-yard graph, the perfect matching and the height function, review related technical facts and state assumptions on graph parameters.

2.1.
Weighted rail-yard graphs

Let
l,r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
l\leq r
. Let

\displaystyle[l..r]:=[l,r]\cap{\mathbb{Z}},

i.e.,
[l..r]
is the set of integers between
l
and
r
. For a positive integer
m
, we use

\displaystyle[m]:=\{1,2,\ldots,m\}.

Consider two binary sequences indexed by integers in
[l..r]

•

the
LR
sequence
\underline{a}=\{a_{l},a_{l+1},\ldots,a_{r}\}\in\{L,R\}^{[l..r]}
;

•

the sign sequence
\underline{b}=(b_{l},b_{l+1},\ldots,b_{r})\in\{+,-\}^{[l..r]}
.

The rail-yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with respect to integers
l
and
r
, the
LR
sequence
\underline{a}
and the sign sequence
\underline{b}
, is the bipartite graph with vertex set
[2l-1..2r+1]\times\left\{{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{1}{2}\right\}
. A vertex is called even (resp. odd) if its abscissa is an even (resp. odd) integer. Each even vertex
(2m,y)
,
m\in[l..r]
is incident to 3 edges, two horizontal edges joining it to the odd vertices
(2m-1,y)
and
(2m+1,y)
and one diagonal edge joining it to

•

the odd vertex
(2m-1,y+1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,+)
;

•

the odd vertex
(2m-1,y-1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,-)
;

•

the odd vertex
(2m+1,y+1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,+)
;

•

the odd vertex
(2m+1,y-1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,-)
.

See Figure
2.1
for an example of a rail-yard graph.

Figure 2.1.
A rail yard graph with LR sequence
\underline{a}=\{L,R,R,L\}
and sign sequence
\underline{b}=\{+,+,-,-\}
. Odd vertices are represented by red points, and even vertices are represented by blue points. Dark lines represent a pure dimer covering. Assume that above the horizontal line
y=4
, only horizontal edges with an odd vertex on the left are present in the dimer configuration; and below the horizontal line
y=-4
, only horizontal edges with an even vertex on the left are present in the dimer configuration. The corresponding sequence of partitions (from the left to the right) is given by
\emptyset\prec(2,0,\ldots)\prec^{\prime}(3,1,1,\ldots)\succ^{\prime}(2,0,\ldots)\succ\emptyset
.

The left boundary (resp. right boundary) of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
consists of all odd vertices with abscissa
2l-1
(resp.
2r+1
). Vertices which do not belong to the boundaries are called inner. A face of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
is called an inner face if it contains only inner vertices.

We assign edge weights to a rail yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
as follows:

•

all the horizontal edges has weight 1; and

•

each diagonal edge adjacent to a vertex with abscissa
2i
has weight
x_{i}
.

2.2.
Dimer coverings and pure dimer coverings

Definition 2.1
.

A dimer covering is a subset of edges of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
such that

(1)

each inner vertex of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
is incident to exactly one edge in the subset;

(2)

each left boundary vertex or right boundary vertex is incident to at most one edge in the subset;

(3)

only a finite number of diagonal edges are present in the subset.

A pure dimer covering of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
is dimer covering of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
satisfying the following two additional conditions

•

each left boundary vertex
(2l-1,y)
is incident to exactly one edge (resp. no edges) in the subset if
y>0
(resp.
y<0
).

•

each right boundary vertex
(2r+1,y)
is incident to exactly one edge (resp. no edges) in the subset if
y<0
(resp.
y>0
).

See Figure
2.1
for an example of pure dimer coverings on a rail yard graph.

For a dimer covering
M
on the rail-yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
, define the associated height function
h_{M}
on faces of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
as follows. We first define a preliminary height function
\overline{h}_{M}
on faces of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
. Note that there exists a positive integer
N>0
, such that when
y<-N
, only horizontal edges with even vertices on the left are present. Fix a face
f_{0}
of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
such that the midpoint of
f_{0}
is on the horizontal line
y=-N
, and define

\displaystyle\overline{h}_{M}(f_{0})=0.

For any two adjacent faces
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
sharing at least one edge,

•

If moving from
f_{1}
to
f_{2}
crosses a present (resp. absent) horizontal edge in
M
with odd vertex on the left, then
\overline{h}_{M}(f_{2})-\overline{h}_{M}(f_{1})=1
(resp.
\overline{h}_{M}(f_{2})-\overline{h}_{M}(f_{1})=-1
).

•

If moving from
f_{1}
to
f_{2}
crosses a present (resp. absent) diagonal edge in
M
with odd vertex on the left, then
\overline{h}_{M}(f_{2})-\overline{h}_{M}(f_{1})=2
(resp.
\overline{h}_{M}(f_{2})-\overline{h}_{M}(f_{1})=0
).

Let
\overline{h}_{0}
be the preliminary height function associated to the dimer configuration satisfying

•

no diagonal edge is present; and

•

each present edge is horizontal with an even vertex on the left.

The height function
h_{M}
associated to
M
is then defined by

(2.1)

\displaystyle h_{M}=\overline{h}_{M}-\overline{h}_{0}.

Let
m\in[l..r]
. Let
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
be a vertical line such that all the horizontal edges and diagonal edges of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
crossed by
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
have odd vertices on the left. Then for each point
\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
in a face of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
, we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle h_{M}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)=2\left[N_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)+N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)\right];

where
N_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the total number of present horizontal edges in
M
crossed by
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
below
y
, and
N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the total number of present diagonal edges in
M
crossed by
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
below
y
. From the definition of a pure dimer covering we can see that both
N_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
and
N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
are finite for each finite
y
.

Note also that
x=2m+\frac{1}{2}
is a vertical line such that all the horizontal edges and diagonal edges of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
crossed by
x=2m+\frac{1}{2}
have even vertices on the left. Then for each point
\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
in a face of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
, we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle h_{M}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)=2\left[J_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)-N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)\right];

where
J_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the total number of absent horizontal edges in
M
crossed by
x=2m+\frac{1}{2}
below
y
, and
N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the total number of present diagonal edges in
M
crossed by
x=2m+\frac{1}{2}
below
y
. From the definition of a pure dimer covering we can also see that both
J_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
and
N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
are finite for each finite
y
.

We define the charge
c^{(M,m)}
on column
(2m-1)
for the configuration
M
as follows:

(2.4)

\displaystyle c^{(M,m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{number\ of\ particles\ on\ column\ }(2m-1)\ \mathrm{in\ the\ upper\ half\ plane}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{number\ of\ holes\ on\ column\ }(2m-1)\ \mathrm{in\ the\ lower\ half\ plane}

2.3.
Partitions

A partition is a non-increasing sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of non-negative integers which vanish eventually. Let
\mathbb{Y}
be the set of all the partitions. The size of a partition is defined by

\displaystyle|\lambda|=\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}.

Two partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
are called interlaced, and written by
\lambda\succ\mu
or
\mu\prec\lambda
if

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\ldots

When representing partitions by Young diagrams, this means
\lambda/\mu
is a horizontal strip. The conjugate partition
\lambda^{\prime}
of
\lambda
is a partition whose Young diagram
Y_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the image of the Young diagram
Y_{\lambda}
of
\lambda
by the reflection along the main diagonal. More precisely

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{\prime}:=\left|\{j\geq 0:\lambda_{j}\geq i\}\right|,\qquad\forall i\geq 1.

We write
\lambda\prec^{\prime}\mu
if
\lambda^{\prime}\prec\mu^{\prime}
.

The skew Schur functions are defined in Section I.5 of
[
33
]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\lambda
,
\mu
be partitions. Define the skew Schur functions as follows

\displaystyle s_{\lambda/\mu}=\det\left(h_{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j}\right)_{i,j=1}^{l(\lambda)}

Here for each
r\geq 0
,
h_{r}
is the
r
th complete symmetric function defined by the sum of all monomials of total degree
r
in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
. More precisely,

\displaystyle h_{r}=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\ldots\leq i_{r}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{r}}

If
r<0
,
h_{r}=0
.

Define the Schur function as follows

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda/\emptyset}.

Let
\lambda
,
\mu
be two partitions. We write
\lambda\supseteq\mu
if
\lambda_{i}\geq\mu_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
. To a sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in{\mathbb{Y}}^{2N+1}
of partitions
(2N+1)
partitions satisfying

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mu^{(0)}\subseteq\mu^{(1)}\supseteq\mu^{(2)}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mu^{(2N-1)}\supseteq\mu^{(2N)}.

We define a probability

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=\frac{u^{|\mu^{(0)}|}v^{|\mu^{(2N)}|}\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}s_{\lambda^{(2k+1)}/\lambda^{(2k)}}(\rho_{2k+1})s_{\lambda^{(2k+2)}/\lambda^{2k}}(\rho_{2k+2})}{Z},

where
Z
is a normalizing constant called the partition function defined by

\displaystyle Z:=\sum_{\mu^{(0)}\subseteq\mu^{(1)}\supseteq\mu^{(2)}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mu^{(2N-1)}\supseteq\mu^{(2N)}}u^{|\mu^{(0)}|}v^{|\mu^{(2N)}|}\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}s_{\lambda^{(2k+1)}/\lambda^{(2k)}}(\rho_{2k+1})s_{\lambda^{(2k+2)}/\lambda^{2k}}(\rho_{2k+2})

The following proposition is proved in Section 5.3 of
[
10
]
, see also Proposition 2.1 of
[
4
]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

The partition function can be computed by

\displaystyle Z=\prod_{1\leq k\leq l\leq N}H(\rho_{2k-1};\rho_{2l})\prod_{n\geq 1}\frac{\widetilde{H}([u^{n-1}v^{n}]\rho^{(o)})\widetilde{H}([u^{n}v^{n-1}]\rho^{(e)})H([u^{2n}]\rho^{(o)};[v^{2n}]\rho^{(e)})}{1-u^{n}v^{n}}

where

\displaystyle\rho^{(o)}=\cup_{1\leq i\leq N}\rho_{2i-1}\qquad\rho^{(e)}=\cup_{1\leq i\leq N}\rho_{2i}

See the Appendix for the definitions of
H
and
\widetilde{H}
.

2.4.
Dimer coverings and sequences of partitions

For a dimer covering
M
of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
, we associate a particle-hole configuration to each odd vertex of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
as follows: let
m\in[l..(r+1)]
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
: if the odd endpoint
\left(2m-1,k+\frac{1}{2}\right)
is incident to a present edge in
M
on its right (resp. left), then associate a hole (resp. particle) to the odd endpoint
\left(2m-1,k+\frac{1}{2}\right)
. When
M
is a pure dimer covering, it is not hard to check that there exists
N>0
, such that when
y>N
, only holes exist and when
y<-N
, only particles exist.

We associate a partition
\lambda^{(M,m)}
to the column indexed by
m
of particle-hole configurations, which corresponds to a pure dimer covering
M
adjacent to odd vertices with abscissa
(2m-1)
as follows. Assume

\displaystyle\lambda^{(M,m)}=(\lambda^{(M,m)}_{1},\lambda^{(M,m)}_{2},\ldots),

Then for
i\geq 1
,
\lambda^{(M,m)}_{i}
is the total number of holes in
M
along the vertical line
x=2m-1
below the
i
th highest particles. Let
l(\lambda^{(M,m)})
be the total number of nonzero parts in the partition
\lambda^{(M,m)}
.

Let
\lambda^{(l)},\lambda^{(r+1)}
be two partitions. The weight of a dimer covering
M
of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
is defined as follows

\displaystyle w(M):=u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}\prod_{i=l}^{r}x_{i}^{d_{i}(M)},

where
d_{i}(M)
is the total number of present diagonal edges of
M
incident to an even vertex with abscissa
2i
.

The partition function
Z_{\lambda^{(l)},\lambda^{(r+1)}}(G,\underline{x})
of dimer coverings on
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
whose configurations on the left (resp. right) boundary correspond to partition
\lambda^{(l)}
(resp.
\lambda^{(r+1)}
) is the sum of weights of all such dimer coverings on the graph. Given the left and right boundary conditions
\lambda^{(l)}
and
\lambda^{(r+1)}
, respectively, the probability of a dimer covering
M
is then defined by

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(M):\propto w(M).

Note that pure dimer coverings have left and right boundary conditions given by

(2.8)

\displaystyle\lambda^{(l)}=\lambda^{(r+1)}=\emptyset;

respectively. The free boundary condition on the left (respectively. right) boundary means that the left (respectively. right) boundary partition can be arbitrary.

The bosonic Fock space
\mathcal{B}
is the infinite dimensional Hilbert space spanned by the orthonormal basis vectors
|\lambda\rangle
, where
\lambda
runs over all the partitions. Let
\langle\lambda|
denote the dual basis vector. Let
x
be a formal or a complex variable. Introduce the operators
\Gamma_{L+}(x)
,
\Gamma_{L-}(x)
,
\Gamma_{R+}(x)
,
\Gamma_{R-}(x)
from
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{B}
as follows

\displaystyle\Gamma_{L+}(x)|\lambda\rangle=\sum_{\mu\prec\lambda}x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}|\mu\rangle;\qquad\Gamma_{R+}(x)|\lambda\rangle=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\prec\lambda^{\prime}}x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}|\mu\rangle;

\displaystyle\Gamma_{L-}(x)|\lambda\rangle=\sum_{\mu\succ\lambda}x^{|\mu|-|\lambda|}|\mu\rangle;\qquad\Gamma_{R-}(x)|\lambda\rangle=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\succ\lambda^{\prime}}x^{|\mu|-|\lambda|}|\mu\rangle;

Such operators were first studied in
[
36
]
for random partitions.

Proof.

See Lemma 2.5 of
[
32
]
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

For each pair of sequences
\underline{a},\underline{b}
, dimer configurations on
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with boundary conditions given by one of the following cases

(a)

The left boundary partition can be arbitrary (free boundary condition); while the right boundary partition is given by the empty partition;

(b)

Both the left boundary partition and the right boundary partition are empty partitions (pure dimer coverings);

(c)

Both the left boundary partition and the right boundary partition are arbitrary;

there exist an integer
N
and a sequence of specializations
u,v,\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{2N}
such that the probability measure of these dimer configurations defined by (
2.7
) is the same as the probability measures on the corresponding sequences of partitions defined by (
2.6
).

Proof.

For each given pair of sequences
\underline{a},\underline{b}
, we may construct a specialization recursively as follows:

(1)

Let
i=l
,
j=1
;

(2)

If one of the following conditions hold

•

j
is odd and
b_{i}=-
; or

•

j
is even and
b_{i}=+
;

set
p_{n}(\rho_{j})=0
, for all
n\geq 1
, then increase
j
by 1.

(3)

If one of the following conditions hold:

•

j
is odd and
b_{i}=-
; or

•

j
is even and
b_{i}=+
;

Then

•

If
a_{i}=L
, let
p_{n}(\rho_{j})=x_{i}^{n}
, where
x_{i}
is the diagonal edge weight on the corresponding column;

•

If
a_{i}=R
, let
p_{n}(\rho_{j})=(-1)^{n+1}x_{i}^{n}
;

Increase both
i
and
j
by 1 and return to step (2).

(4)

The process stops at
i=r
;

•

If
j
is even when
i=r
, set
N=\frac{j}{2}
;

•

If
j
is odd when
i=r
, set
N=\frac{j+1}{2}
, and let
p_{n}(\rho_{2N})=0
for all
n\geq 1
.

In case (a), we let
v=0
; in case (c), we let
u=v=0
, then the lemma follows.
∎

Proposition 2.5
.

(1)

The partition function for dimer coverings on a real yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with the free boundary condition on both the left boundary and the right boundary is given by

(2.9)

\displaystyle Z_{f,f}=\prod_{l\leq i<j\leq r;b_{i}=+,b_{j}=-}z_{i,j}\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{\prod_{i\in[l..r],b_{i}=+}(1-u^{n-1}v^{n}x_{i})\prod_{i\in[l..r],b_{i}=-}(1-u^{n}v^{n-1}x_{i})}\right]

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\frac{\prod_{l\leq i,j\leq r;b_{i}=+,b_{j}=-,a_{i}\neq a_{j}}(1+u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i}x_{j})}{(1-u^{n}v^{n})\prod_{l\leq i,j\leq r;b_{i}=+,b_{j}=-,a_{i}=a_{j}}(1-u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i}x_{j})}\right]

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\frac{\prod_{i<j\in[l..r],b_{i}=b_{j}=+,a_{i}\neq a_{j}}(1+u^{2n-2}v^{2n}x_{i}x_{j})\prod_{i<j\in[l..r],b_{i}=b_{j}=-,a_{i}\neq a_{j}}(1+u^{2n}v^{2n-2}x_{i}x_{j})}{\prod_{i<j\in[l..r],b_{i}=b_{j}=+,a_{i}\neq a_{j}}(1-u^{2n-2}v^{2n}x_{i}x_{j})\prod_{i<j\in[l..r],b_{i}=b_{j}=-,a_{i}=a_{j}}(1-u^{2n}v^{2n-2}x_{i}x_{j})}\right]

where

(2.10)

\displaystyle z_{ij}=\begin{cases}1+x_{i}x_{j}&\mathrm{if}\ a_{i}\neq a_{j}\\
\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}&\mathrm{if}\ a_{i}=a_{j}\end{cases}

(2)

The partition function for dimer coverings on a real yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with the free boundary condition on the left boundary and the empty partition on the right boundary is given by

(2.11)

\displaystyle Z_{f,\emptyset}=\prod_{l\leq i<j\leq r;b_{i}=+,b_{j}=-}z_{i,j}\left[\frac{1}{\prod_{i\in[l..r],b_{i}=-}(1-ux_{i})}\right]\left[\frac{\prod_{i<j\in[l..r],b_{i}=b_{j}=-,a_{i}\neq a_{j}}(1+u^{2}x_{i}x_{j})}{\prod_{i<j\in[l..r],b_{i}=b_{j}=-,a_{i}=a_{j}}(1-u^{2}x_{i}x_{j})}\right]

Proof.

Part (1) follows from Proposition
2.3
and Lemma
2.4
. Part (2) follows from Part (1) by setting
v=0
.
∎

Now we discuss the assumptions in order to obtain the asymptotical result.

Assumption 2.6
.

Let
\{RYG(l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)},\underline{a}^{(\epsilon)},\underline{b}^{(\epsilon)})\}_{\epsilon>0}
be a sequence of rail-yard graphs with the weights of diagonal edges incident with
x=2i
given with
x_{i}^{(\epsilon)}
.

(1)

Piecewise periodicity of the graph.
For a positive integer
n
and real numbers
V_{0}<V_{1}<\ldots<V_{m}
, we say that a given sequence of rail-yard graphs is
n
-periodic with transition points
V_{0},V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
if

(a)

For each
\epsilon>0
, there exist integer multiples of
n

\displaystyle l^{(\epsilon)}=v_{0}^{(\epsilon)}<v_{1}^{(\epsilon)}<\ldots<v_{m}^{(\epsilon)}=r^{(\epsilon)},

such that
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\epsilon v_{p}^{(\epsilon)}=V_{p},\ \forall p\in\{0\}\cup[m].

(b)

The sequence
\underline{a}^{(\epsilon)}
is
n
-periodic on
\left[l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)}\right]
and does not depend on
\epsilon
. More precisely, there exist
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\in\{L,R\}
such that

\displaystyle a_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=a_{i_{\equiv_{n}}},

where
i_{\equiv_{n}}\in[n]
is in the same congruence class modulo
n
as
i
.

(c)

For each
p\in[m]
, the sequence

\underline{b}^{(\epsilon)}
is
n
-periodic on
(v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)},v_{p}^{(\epsilon)})
and does not depend on
\epsilon
, but it may depend on
p
. More precisely, there exist
b_{p,1},b_{p,2},\dots,b_{p,n}\in\{+,-\}
such that for
i\in(v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)},v_{p}^{(\epsilon)})

\displaystyle b_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=b_{p,i_{\equiv_{n}}}.

(2)

Periodicity of weights.
The weights
x_{i}^{(\epsilon)}
are periodic q-volume weights. Precisely they are given with

\displaystyle x_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=\begin{cases}e^{-\epsilon(i-i_{\equiv_{n}})}\tau_{k},&b_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=+\\
e^{\epsilon(i-i_{\equiv_{n}})}\tau^{-1}_{k},&b_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=-\end{cases}.

(3)

Limit regime.

(a)

Assume that
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}t=1
in a such a way that

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}-\frac{\log t}{n\epsilon}=\beta,

where
\beta
is a positive real number independent of
\epsilon
.

(b)

Let
s
be a positive integer and assume that for
d\in[s]
the sequence
i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}=\chi_{d},

for
\chi_{1}\leq\chi_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\chi_{s}
and that
i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\,\mathrm{mod}\,n
does not depend on
\epsilon
, i.e. that there exist
i^{*}_{1},i^{*}_{2},\dots,i^{*}_{s}\in[n]
such that

\displaystyle(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)})_{\equiv_{n}}=i^{*}_{d}.

If
s=1
we drop the index, i.e. we assume that
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\epsilon i^{(\epsilon)}=\chi
and
(i^{(\epsilon)})_{\equiv_{n}}=i^{*}
.

2.5.
Main Results

In this section we state the main results proved in this paper. For simplicity, the details of certain notation are explained in later sections. Theorem
2.7
is about the limit shape. More precisely, it says that in the scaling limit, the rescaled height function converges to a deterministic function, which gives a 2D analogue of the law of large numbers.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
M
be a random dimer covering on the rail yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with probability distribution given by (
2.7
) and (
2.8
). Let
h_{M}
be the height function associated to
M
as defined in (
2.1
).
Suppose Assumption
2.6
holds.
Then the rescaled random height function
\epsilon h_{M}\left(\frac{\chi}{\epsilon},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)
converges, as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, to a non-random function
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)
such that the Laplace transform of
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\cdot)
is given by

(2.12)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-n\alpha\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)d\kappa=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-n\alpha\kappa}\epsilon h_{M}\left(\frac{\chi}{\epsilon},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)d\kappa=\frac{1}{n^{2}\alpha^{2}\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\alpha}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\alpha},

where the contour
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
4.5
,
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)
and
\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)
are defined by (
4.12
) and (
4.13
). Here

\displaystyle[\zeta]^{\alpha}=e^{\alpha\log[\zeta]}

and the branch of
\log(\zeta)
is chosen to be real positive when
\zeta
is real positive.
Note that the right hand side is non-random.

Simulations of perfect matchings show that in the scaling limit, there are certain regions where one type of edges are a.s. to be present in a perfect matching; while other types of edges a.s. do not occur; these are called “frozen regions”. Definition
2.8
gives a precise definition.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\{RYG(l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)},\underline{a}^{(\epsilon)},\underline{b}^{(\epsilon)})\}_{\epsilon>0}
be a collection of rail-yard graphs satisfying Assumption
2.6
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)
be the limit of the rescaled height function of free boundary dimer coverings on rail yard graphs as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, as obtained in Lemma
2.7
. The liquid region for the limit shape of pure dimer coverings on these rail yard graphs as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}:=\left\{(\chi,\kappa)\in(l^{(0)},r^{(0)})\times{\mathbb{R}}:\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial\kappa}(\chi,\kappa)\in(0,2)\right\}.

and the frozen region is defined to be

\displaystyle\left\{(\chi,\kappa)\in(l^{(0)},r^{(0)})\times{\mathbb{R}}:\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial\kappa}(\chi,\kappa)\in\{0,2\}\right\}.

The frozen boundary is defined to be the boundary separating the frozen region and the liquid region.

We also obtain explicit equations for the frozen boundary; see Theorem
2.9
. Again for simplicity technical assumptions are delayed to later sections.

Theorem 2.9
.

Suppose Assumption
2.6
and (
4.6
) hold. Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
hold. Then
(\chi,\kappa)\in(l^{(0)},r^{(0)})\times{\mathbb{R}}
is on the frozen boundary if and only if
(\chi,\kappa)
satisfies the following system of equations for some
w\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}

(2.13)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=e^{-n\kappa}.\\
\frac{d\log[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]}{dw}=0.\end{cases}

Theorem
2.10
says that the unrescaled height function converges to the restriction to a subset of the upper half plane
{\mathbb{H}}
of the Gaussian free field (GFF) in
{\mathbb{H}}
, which gives a version of the central limit theorem.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\{RYG(l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)},\underline{a}^{(\epsilon)},\underline{b}^{(\epsilon)})\}_{\epsilon>0}
be a sequence of rail-yard graphs satisfying Assumptions
2.6
,
5.1
. Let
\mathbf{w}_{+}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathbf{w}_{+}(\mathcal{L})\subset{\mathbb{H}}
be the diffeomorphism from the liquid region to a subset of the upper half plane which maps each point
(\chi,\kappa)
in the liquid region to the unique root of (
5.12
) in the upper half plane
{\mathbb{H}}
. Then as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, the height function of pure dimer coverings on
\{RYG(l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)},\underline{a}^{(\epsilon)},\underline{b}^{(\epsilon)})\}_{\epsilon>0}
in the liquid region converges to the
\mathbf{w}_{+}
-pullback of the restriction to
\mathbf{w}_{+}(\mathcal{L})
of the GFF in
{\mathbb{H}}
in the sense that for any
(\chi,\kappa)\in\mathcal{L}
,
\chi\notin\{V_{p}\}_{p=0}^{m}
and positive integer
k

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(h_{M}\left(\frac{\chi}{\epsilon},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[h_{M}\left(\frac{\chi}{\epsilon},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)\right]\right)e^{-n\beta k\kappa}d\kappa\longrightarrow\int_{(\chi,\kappa)\in\mathcal{L}}e^{-n\kappa k\beta}\Xi(\mathbf{w}_{+}(\chi,\kappa))d\kappa

in distribution, where
\Xi
is the GFF in
{\mathbb{H}}
as defined in Section
6.1
.

3.
Moment Formula for Height Function

In this section, we compute the moments of height functions of perfect matchings on rail-yard graphs by computing the observables in a Macdonald processes with dual specialization.

3.1.
Height function and Laplace transform

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
M
be a dimer covering of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
. Let
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h_{M}(x,y)t^{k(y-c^{(M,m)})}dy=\frac{2}{(k\log t)^{2}}\left[t^{-kl(\lambda^{(M,m)})}+(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda^{(M,m)})}t^{k(\lambda_{i}^{(M,m)}-i+1)}\right].

Proof.

Following the same argument as in Section 2.4 of
[
32
]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h_{M}(x,y)t^{ky}dy=\frac{2t^{c^{(M,m)}k}}{(k\log t)^{2}}\left[t^{-kl(\lambda^{(M,m)})}+(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda^{(M,m)})}t^{k(\lambda_{i}^{(M,m)}-i+1)}\right].

Then the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
M
be a dimer covering of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
. Then for each
m\in[l..r]
, the central charge
c^{(M,m)}
is a constant independent of
m
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the central charges on any two consecutive columns are equal; i.e.

(3.1)

\displaystyle c^{(M,m)}=c^{(M,m+1)};\ \forall m\in[l..r-1].

Let
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Consider the odd vertices
v_{1}=(2m-1,i+\frac{1}{2})
and
v_{2}=(2m+1,i+\frac{1}{2})
. Let
\Phi(M)
be the particle-hole configuration on odd vertices induced by
M
. Let
w=(2m,i+\frac{1}{2})
be the even vertex between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. The following cases might occur

(1)

(a)

(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,-)
; let
w_{1}:=w+(0,1)

(b)

(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,+)
; let
w_{1}:=w-(0,1)

When
a_{m}=L
:

•

If both the diagonal edges adjacent to
w
and
w_{1}
are present in
M
; in this case both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have holes in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If neither the diagonal edges adjacent to
w
nor
w_{1}
are present in
M
; in this case either both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have holes in
\Phi(M)
; or both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have particles in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If the diagonal edge adjacent to
w
is present in
M
and the diagonal edge
w_{1}
is absent in
M
; in this case
v_{1}
has a particle and
v_{2}
has a hole in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If the diagonal edge adjacent to
w_{1}
is present in
M
and the diagonal edge
w
is absent in
M
; in this case
v_{1}
has a hole and
v_{2}
has a particle in
\Phi(M)
;

See Figure
3.1
for the case when
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,-)
, and Figure
3.2
for the case when
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,+)
.

Figure 3.1.
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,-)
: at odd vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, particles are represented by grey disks and holes are represented by circles.

Figure 3.2.
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,+)

(2)

(a)

(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,-)
; let
w_{1}:=w+(0,1)

(b)

(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,+)
; let
w_{1}:=w-(0,1)

When
a_{m}=R
:

•

If both the diagonal edges adjacent to
w
and
w_{1}
are present in
M
; in this case both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have particles in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If neither the diagonal edges adjacent to
w
nor
w_{1}
are present in
M
; in this case either both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have holes in
\Phi(M)
; or both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have particles in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If the diagonal edge adjacent to
w
is present in
M
and the diagonal edge
w_{1}
is absent in
M
; in this case
v_{1}
has a particle and
v_{2}
has a hole in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If the diagonal edge adjacent to
w_{1}
is present in
M
and the diagonal edge
w
is absent in
M
; in this case
v_{1}
has a hole and
v_{2}
has a particle in
\Phi(M)
;

See Figure
3.3
for the case when
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,-)
, and Figure
3.4
for the case when
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,+)
.

Figure 3.3.
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,-)

Figure 3.4.
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,+)

By the definitions of dimer coverings on a rail-yard graph (Definition
2.1
), there are only finitely many present diagonal edges in
M
. Hence we may translate the horizontal axis such that all the present diagonal edges are in the upper half plane. For each
m\in[l..r]
, let
\mathcal{D}_{M,m}
be the set of all the present diagonal edges in
M
incident to an even vertex with abscissa
2m
. Then there exists a finite positive integer
s
such that we can write
D_{M,m}
into the following disjoint union:

\displaystyle D_{M,m}=D_{M,m}^{(1)}\cup D_{M,m}^{(2)}\cup\ldots\cup D_{M,m}^{(s)}.

such that

•

For
1\leq i<j\leq s
,
D_{M,m}^{(i)}\cap D_{M,m}^{(j)}=\emptyset
;

•

D_{(M,m)}^{(i)}
consists of all the diagonal edges adjacent to even vertices with ordinate in the set
\{p+\frac{1}{2}\}_{p\in[p_{i,-}..p_{i,+}]}
, where

–

p_{1,-}\leq p_{1,+}<p_{2,-}\leq p_{2,+}<\ldots<p_{s,-}\leq p_{s,+}
are integers.

–

p_{i,+}+1<p_{i+1,-}
for all
i\in[l-1]
.

For each
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
v_{1}^{(j)}
,
v_{2}^{(j)}
be two odd vertices given by

\displaystyle v_{1}^{(j)}:=\left(2m-1,j+\frac{1}{2}\right)\qquad v_{2}^{(j)}:=\left(2m+1,j+\frac{1}{2}\right)

Moreover for
i\in[s]
, define

\displaystyle q_{i,-}=\begin{cases}p_{i,-}&\mathrm{if}\ b_{m}=+\\
p_{i,-}-1&\mathrm{if}\ b_{m}=-\end{cases}\qquad q_{i,+}=\begin{cases}p_{i,+}+1&\mathrm{if}\ b_{m}=+\\
p_{i,-}&\mathrm{if}\ b_{m}=-\end{cases}

By cases (1) and (2) listed above, we obtain

•

For
j\notin\{q_{i,-},q_{i,+}\}_{i\in[s]}
; then either both
v_{1}^{(j)}
and
v_{2}^{(j)}
have particles in
\Phi(M)
, or both
v_{1}^{(j)}
and
v_{2}^{(j)}
have holes in
\Phi(M)
.

•

If
b_{m}=+
: then for each
i\in[s]
,
v_{1}^{(q_{i,-})}
has a particle in
\Phi(M)
,

v_{2}^{(q_{i,-})}
has a hole in
\Phi(M)
;

v_{1}^{(q_{i,+})}
has a hole in
\Phi(M)
,

v_{2}^{(q_{i,+})}
has a particle in
\Phi(M)
;

•

If
b_{m}=-
: then for each
i\in[s]
,
v_{1}^{(q_{i,-})}
has a hole in
\Phi(M)
,

v_{2}^{(q_{i,-})}
has a particle in
\Phi(M)
;

v_{1}^{(q_{i,+})}
has a particle in
\Phi(M)
,

v_{2}^{(q_{i,+})}
has a hole in
\Phi(M)

Then it follows that
c^{(M,m)}=c^{(M,m+1)}
when all the present diagonal edges are in the upper half plane. It is straightforward to check that (
3.1
) still holds if we vertically translate the
x
-axis of the coordinate system. Then the lemma follows.
∎

When considering dimer coverings on a rail-yard graph, translating each dimer covering vertically does not change the corresponding sequence of partitions; and such translations altogether are counted exactly once in the free boundary condition partition function
Z_{f,f}
given by (
2.9
). Hence we only need to consider the height function for a specific translation
M
for which the central charge vanishes, i.e.
c^{(M,m)}=0
for all
m\in[l..r]
. In this case by Lemma
3.1
we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h_{M}(x,y)t^{ky}dy=\frac{2}{(k\log t)^{2}}\left[t^{-kl(\lambda^{(M,m)})}+(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda^{(M,m)})}t^{k(\lambda_{i}^{(M,m)}-i+1)}\right].

3.2.
Macdonald Processes

From Lemma
2.4
, we see that in order to study the probability measure for dimer coverings on rail-yard graphs with free boundary conditions, it suffices to study the corresponding probability measures on sequences of partitions (
2.5
) given by (
2.6
).

Now we recall the definition of a Macdonald process, which is a formal probability measure on sequences of partitions such that the probability of each sequence of partitions is proportional to a sum of products of skew Macdonald polynomials.

Definition 3.3
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(A^{(l)},A^{(l+1)},\ldots,A^{(r)},A^{(r+1)});

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(B^{(l)},B^{(l+1)},\ldots,B^{(r)},B^{(r+1)}).

be
2(r-l+2)
set of variables, in which each
A^{(i)}
or
B^{(j)}
consists of countably many variables. Let
u,v
be two variables.
Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}\}
be a partition of the set
[l..r]
, i.e.

(1)

\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{R}=[l..r]
; and

(2)

\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset
.

Define a formal probability measure on sequences of
(r-l+2)
partitions

\displaystyle(\lambda^{(l)},\lambda^{(r+1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r)},\lambda^{(r+1)})

with respect to
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
and parameters
q,t\in(0,1)
by

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{MP}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathcal{P},q,t}(\lambda^{(l)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r+1)})\propto u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{L}}\Psi_{\lambda^{(i)},\lambda^{(i+1)}}(A^{(i)},B^{(i+1)};q,t)\right]\left[\prod_{j\in\mathcal{R}}\Phi_{[\lambda^{(j)}]^{\prime},[\lambda^{(j+1)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(j)},B^{(j+1)};q,t)\right]

where for two partitions
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, and two countable set of variables
A
,
B
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda/\nu}(A;q,t)Q_{\mu/\nu}(B;q,t).

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}Q_{\lambda/\nu}(A;t,q)P_{\mu/\nu}(B;t,q)

See Section
A
for definitions of Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}
,
Q_{\lambda}
,
P_{\lambda/\mu}
,
Q_{\lambda/\mu}
.

Remark 3.4
.

In terms of the scalar product as defined in (
A.5
), we also have

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)=\langle P_{\lambda}(A,Y;q,t),Q_{\mu}(Y,B;q,t)\rangle_{Y},

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B)=\langle P_{\mu}(Y,B;t,q),Q_{\lambda}(A,Y;t,q)\rangle_{Y}

where
Y
is a countable set of variables.

Lemma 3.5
.

Consider free boundary dimer coverings on the rail-yard graph with probability measure given by (
2.7
). Then the corresponding sequences of partitions form a generalized Macdonald process as in Definition
3.3
with

(1)

\mathcal{L}=\{i\in[l..r]:a_{i}=L\}
and
\mathcal{R}=\{j\in[l..r]:a_{j}=R\}
; and

(2)

For
i\in[l..r]
,

(a)

if
b_{i}=-
, then
A^{(i)}=\{x_{i}\},B^{(i+1)}=\{0\}
;

(b)

if
b_{i}=+
, then
A^{(i)}=\{0\},B^{(i+1)}=\{x_{i}\}
;

(3)

q=t.

Proof.

Similar argument as the proof of Lemma 3.3 in
[
32
]
.
∎

Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
be a partition and
q,t\in(0,1)
be parameters. Let

(3.3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\lambda;q,t)=(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}q^{k\lambda_{i}}t^{k(-i+1)}+t^{-kl(\lambda)}

Let

\displaystyle\Pi_{L,L}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\Pi(X,Y;q,t)

(3.4)

\displaystyle\Pi_{R,R}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\Pi(X,Y;t,q)

\displaystyle\Pi_{L,R}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{R,L}(X,Y)=\Pi^{\prime}(X,Y).

where
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
are defined by (
A.11
). Let

Lemma 3.6
.

For any countable set of variables
X
,
c\in\{R,L\}
, define specializations

\displaystyle\rho_{X,1,c}(p_{k}):

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sigma(c)}p_{k}[(-1)^{\sigma(c)}X];

\displaystyle\rho_{X,t,2,c}(p_{k}):

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sigma(c)}\left(1-t^{(2\sigma(c)-1)k}\right)p_{k}((-1)^{\sigma(c)}t^{1-2\sigma(c)}X^{-1})].

where

\displaystyle\sigma(c)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if}\ c=L;\\
1&\mathrm{if}\ c=R\end{cases}

Let
\Pi
,
\Pi^{\prime}
, and
L
be as in (
A.11
) and (
A.9
). Let
H(\cdot;\cdot)
be defined as in (
A.3
), then

\displaystyle\rho_{X,t,2,c}=t^{1-2\sigma(a_{i})}\rho_{X^{-1},1,c}\cup(-1)*\rho_{X^{-1},1,c},

\displaystyle\Pi(X,Y;t,t)=H(\rho_{X,1,c};\rho_{Y,1,c}),

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}(X,Y)=H(\rho_{X,1,c};\rho_{Y,1,c^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle\left(L(W,(-1)^{\delta_{c,d}-1}A;\omega(t,t,c))\right)^{(-1)^{\delta_{c,d}-1}}=H(\rho_{W,t,2,c};\rho_{A,1,d});

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{c,d}(B,W)}{\Pi_{c,d}(B,\xi(t,t,c)W)}=H((-1)*\rho_{W^{-1},t^{-1},2,c};\rho_{B,1,d})

where

(3.5)

\displaystyle\omega(q,t,a_{i})=\begin{cases}(q,t)&\mathrm{if}\ a_{i}=L\\
\left(\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)&\mathrm{if}\ a_{i}=R\end{cases},\quad\xi(q,t,a_{j})=\begin{cases}q^{-1}&\mathrm{If}\ a_{j}=L\\
t&\mathrm{If}\ a_{j}=R\end{cases},

Proof.

The lemma follows from Lemma

.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
l_{i}
be non-negative integers for
i\in[l+1..r]
. Define specializations

\displaystyle\rho_{A}

\displaystyle=\cup_{i=l}^{r}\rho_{A^{(i)},1,a_{i}};\qquad\rho_{B}=\cup_{i=l+1}^{r+1}\rho_{B^{(i)},1,a_{i-1}};

\displaystyle\rho_{W}

\displaystyle=\cup_{i=l+1}^{r}\rho_{W^{(i)},t,2,a_{i}};\qquad\rho_{W}^{\circ}=\cup_{i=l+1}^{r}(-1)*\rho_{[W^{(i)]^{-1}},t^{-1},2,a_{i}}.

Then

\displaystyle\left.{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{A,B,\mathcal{P};q,t}}\left[\prod_{i\in[l+1..r]}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\right|_{q=t}

\displaystyle=\oint\ldots\oint\prod_{i=[l+1..r]}D(W^{(i)};\omega(t,t,a_{i}))\times\prod_{i\leq j;i,j\in[l+1..r]}H(\rho_{W^{(i)},t,2,a_{i}};\rho_{A^{(j)},1,a_{j}})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i<j;i,j\in[l..r]}H((-1)*\rho_{[W^{(j)}]^{-1},t^{-1},2,a_{j}};\rho_{B^{(i+1)},1,a_{i}})\times\prod_{i<j;i,j\in[l+1..r]}T_{a_{i},a_{j}}(W^{(i)},W^{(j)})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{k\geq 1}\widetilde{H}([u^{k-1}v^{k}]\rho_{W})\widetilde{H}([u^{k}v^{k-1}]\rho_{W}^{\circ})H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W})H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k-2}\rho_{W}^{\circ}))

\displaystyle\times\prod_{k\geq 1}H(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W}^{\circ})H(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W}))H(u^{2k}\rho_{W};v^{2k}\rho_{W}^{\circ})

where the expectation is with respect to probability measure on dimer configurations of
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with free boundary conditions as defined in (
2.7
);
and

\displaystyle T_{c,d}(Z,W):=\begin{cases}\prod_{z_{i}\in Z}\prod_{w_{j}\in W}\frac{(1-w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})^{2}}{(1-t^{-1}w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})(1-tw_{j}z_{i}^{-1})}&\mathrm{if}\ c=d\\
\prod_{z_{i}\in Z}\prod_{w_{j}\in W}\frac{(1+tw_{j}z_{i}^{-1})^{2}}{(1+t^{2}w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})(1+w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})}&\mathrm{if}\ c=L\ \mathrm{and}\ d=R\\
\prod_{z_{i}\in Z}\prod_{w_{j}\in W}\frac{(1+t^{-1}w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})^{2}}{(1+t^{-2}w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})(1+w_{j}z_{i}^{-1})}&\mathrm{if}\ c=R\ \mathrm{and}\ d=L\end{cases},

where
|W^{(i)}|=l_{i}
, and the integral contours are given by
\{\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[l+1..r],s\in[l_{i}]}
such that

(1)

\mathcal{C}_{i,s}
is the integral contour for the variable
w^{(i)}_{s}\in W^{(i)}
;

(2)

\mathcal{C}_{i,s}
encloses 0 and every singular point of

\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j;i,j\in[l+1..r]}H(\rho_{W^{(i)},t,2,a_{i}};\rho_{A^{(j)},1,a_{j}})\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W}))H(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W})\right],

but no other singular points of the integrand.

(3)

the contour
\mathcal{C}_{i,j}
is contained in the domain bounded by
t\mathcal{C}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
whenever
(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
in lexicographical ordering;

D(W;q,t)
,
H(W,X;q,t)
, and
\Pi_{c,d}(X,Y)
are given by (
A.8
), (
A.9
), and (
3.4
).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left.{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{A,B,\mathcal{P};q,t}}\left[\prod_{i\in[l+1..r]}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\right|_{q=t}

\displaystyle=\left.{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{A,B,\mathcal{P};q,t}}\left[\prod_{i\in[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{L}}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\left[\prod_{i\in[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{R}}\gamma_{l_{i}}\left(\left[\lambda^{(i)}\right]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)\right]\right|_{q=t}

Recall that the Macdonald polynomials satisfy (See Page 324 of
[
33
]
)

(3.6)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)=P_{\lambda}\left(X;\frac{1}{q},\frac{1}{t}\right);\quad Q_{\lambda}(X;q,t)=\left(\frac{t}{q}\right)^{|\lambda|}Q_{\lambda}\left(X;\frac{1}{q},\frac{1}{t}\right)

We obtain

\displaystyle\left.{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{A,B,\mathcal{P};q,t}}\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{L}}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{R}}\gamma_{l_{i}}\left(\left[\lambda^{(i)}\right]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)\right]\right|_{q=t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{\lambda^{(l)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r+1)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{L}}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{R}}\gamma_{l_{i}}\left(\left[\lambda^{(i)}\right]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}\left[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{L}}\langle P_{\lambda^{(i)}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};q,t),Q_{\lambda^{(i+1)}}(Y^{(i)},B^{(i+1)};q,t)\rangle_{Y^{(i)}}\right]

\displaystyle\left.\left[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{R}}\langle Q_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}\left(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};t,q\right),P_{[\lambda^{(i+1)}]^{\prime}}\left(Y^{(i)},B^{(i+1)};t,q\right)\rangle_{Y^{(i)}}\right]\right|_{q=t}

where for each
i
,
Y^{(i)}
is a countable collection of variables; and the partition function
Z
can be computed by Proposition
2.5
.

For
i\in[l+1..r]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{i}=\begin{cases}{\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)P_{\lambda^{(i)}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};q,t)Q_{\lambda^{(i)}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};q,t)&\begin{subarray}{c}(i-1,i)\\
\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{l_{i}}([\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q})Q_{\lambda^{(i)}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};q,t)Q_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};t,q)&\begin{subarray}{c}(i-1,i)\\
\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)P_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};t,q)P_{\lambda^{(i)}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};q,t)&\begin{subarray}{c}(i-1,i)\\
\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{l_{i}}([\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q})P_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};t,q)Q_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};t,q)&\begin{subarray}{c}(i-1,i)\\
\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{l}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{\lambda^{(l)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}P_{\lambda^{(l)}}(A^{(l)},Y^{(l)};q,t)&l\in\mathcal{L}\\
\sum_{\lambda^{(l)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}Q_{[\lambda^{(l)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(l)},Y^{(l)};t,q)&l\in\mathcal{R}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{r+1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{\lambda^{(r+1)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}P_{[\lambda^{(r+1)}]^{\prime}}(Y^{(r)},B^{(r+1)};t,q)&\ r\in\mathcal{R}\\
\sum_{\lambda^{(r+1)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}Q_{\lambda^{(r+1)}}(Y^{(r)},B^{(r+1)};q,t)&r\in\mathcal{L}\end{cases}.

Then

\displaystyle\left.{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{A,B,\mathcal{P},q,t}}\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{L}}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{R}}\gamma_{l_{i}}\left(\left[\lambda^{(i)}\right]^{\prime};t,q\right)\right]\right|_{q=t}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\langle\mathbf{E}_{l}\langle\mathbf{E}_{l+1}\ldots\langle\mathbf{E}_{r},\mathbf{E}_{r+1}\rangle_{Y^{((r))}}\ldots\rangle_{Y^{(l+1)}}\rangle_{Y^{(l)}}\right|_{q=t}

Observe that for
i\in[l+1..r]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{i}=\begin{cases}D_{-l_{i},(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)});q,t}\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}((A^{(i)},Y^{(i)}),(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)}))&\mathrm{If}\ a_{i}=L;\\
D_{-l_{i},(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)});\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}}\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}((A^{(i)},Y^{(i)}),(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)}))&\mathrm{If}\ a_{i}=R;\end{cases}

By Example 4 in Chapter I.5 of
[

]

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{l}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{r+1}

By Proposition

, we obtain for

and

,

•

;

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{i}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}((A^{(i)},Y^{(i)}),(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)}))

\displaystyle\oint\cdots\oint D\left(W^{(i)};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)L\left(W^{(i)},(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)});\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)\frac{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}((Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)}),W^{(i)})}{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}((Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)}),tW^{(i)})};

where each integral contour encloses

and all the singular points of

We interchange the order of the residue and Macdonald scalar product and obtain

\displaystyle\left.{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{A,B,\mathcal{P};q,t}}\left[\prod_{i=[l+1..r]}\gamma_{l_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]\right|_{q=t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\left(\prod_{i=l+1}^{r}\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(A^{(i)},B^{(i)})\right)\oint\langle F_{l}\langle F_{l+1}\ldots\langle F_{r},F_{r+1}\rangle_{Y^{(r)}}\rangle_{Y^{(l+1)}}\rangle_{Y^{(l)}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{L}}D(W^{(i)};q,t)L(W^{(i)},A^{(i)};q,t)\frac{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(B^{(i)},W^{(i)})}{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(B^{(i)},q^{-1}W^{(i)})}\right)

\displaystyle\left.\times\left(\prod_{i=[l+1..r]\cap\mathcal{R}}D\left(W^{(i)};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)L\left(W^{(i)},A^{(i)};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)\frac{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(B^{(i)},W^{(i)})}{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(B^{(i)},tW^{(i)})}\right)\right|_{q=t};

Moreover for
i\in[l+1..r]
,

•

If

\displaystyle F_{i}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i-1)})\cdot\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(Y^{(i-1)},Y^{(i)})\cdot\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(Y^{(i)},B^{(i)})

\displaystyle\times L(W^{(i)},Y^{(i)};t,t)\frac{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(Y^{(i-1)},W^{(i)})}{\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(Y^{(i-1)},q^{-1}W^{(i)})}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i-1)})\cdot\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(Y^{(i-1)},Y^{(i)})\cdot\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}(Y^{(i)},B^{(i)})

and

\displaystyle F_{l}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{l}=\widetilde{H}(\rho_{(uA^{(l)},uY^{(l)}),1,a_{l}})

\displaystyle F_{r+1}

\displaystyle\frac{H(\rho_{Y^{(i)},1,a_{i}},\rho_{t^{1-2\sigma(a_{i})}(W^{(i)})^{-1},1,a_{i}})}{H(\rho_{Y^{(i)},1,a_{i}},\rho_{(W^{(i)})^{-1},1,a_{i}})}\frac{H(\rho_{Y^{(i-1)},1,a_{i-1}},\rho_{W^{(i)},1,a_{i}})}{H(\rho_{Y^{(i-1)},1,a_{i-1}},\rho_{2^{2\sigma(a_{r})-1}W^{(i)},1,a_{i}})}

By Lemmas

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}(\rho_{vB^{(r+1)},1,a_{r}}\cup\rho_{vB^{(r)},1,a_{r-1}}\cup\rho_{vW^{(r)},2,a_{r}}\cup\rho_{vY^{(r-1)}),1,a_{r-1}})

\displaystyle H\left(\rho_{Y^{(r-1)},1,a_{r-1}};\rho_{A^{(r)},1,a_{r}}\right)\frac{H(\rho_{Y^{(r-1)},1,a_{r-1}},\rho_{W^{(r)},1,a_{r}})}{H(\rho_{Y^{(r-1)},1,a_{r-1}},\rho_{t^{2\sigma(a_{r})-1}W^{(r)},a_{r}})}

For
