and

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j_{2},p_{2},<,0}}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\max\{u,v\}<e^{V_{p_{2}-1}-V_{p_{1}-1}}\tau_{j_{2}}^{-1}\tau_{j_{1}}

Lemma 5.4
.

If both Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
hold, then Assumption
5.1
holds.

Proof.

For each integer
i
satisfying
5\leq i\leq 8
, and
k_{i,1},k_{i,2}\geq 1
let

\displaystyle y_{i,k_{i,1},k_{i,2}}\in\mathcal{R}_{i,1,k_{i,1},k_{i,2}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{i,2,k_{i,1},k_{i,2}}.

and for each integer
j
satisfying
1\leq j\leq 4
, let

(5.2)

\displaystyle y_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{j,2}

It follows from Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
that

\displaystyle y_{8,k_{8,1},k_{8,2}}<y_{4}<y_{3}<y_{7,k_{7,1},k_{7,2}}<0<y_{5,k_{5,1},k_{5,2}}<y_{1}<y_{2}<y_{6,k_{6,1},k_{6,2}}

More precisely,
y_{4}<y_{3}<0<y_{1}<y_{2}
follows from Assumption
5.2
(2), and all the others follows from Assumption
5.3
.
Moreover, by Assumption
5.3
we obtain that for
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle y_{5,k,k+1}<y_{5,k,k}<y_{5,k-1,k};\qquad y_{6,k,k+1}>y_{6,k,k}>y_{5,k-1,k}.

\displaystyle y_{7,k,k+1}>y_{7,k,k}>y_{7,k-1,k};\qquad y_{8,k,k+1}<y_{8,k,k}<y_{8,k-1,k}.

Note that (
5.1
) follows from
y_{4}<y_{3}<0<y_{1}<y_{2}
.

For each integer
j
satisfying
1\leq j\leq 4
, let
z_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j,2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{j,1}
. Let
y_{j}
satisfy (
5.2
). Then
y_{1}
and
z_{1}
have the following form

\displaystyle y_{1}=e^{\max\{V_{p_{2}-1},\chi\}}\tau_{j_{*}}^{-1};\qquad z_{1}=e^{V_{p_{1}}}\tau_{i_{*}}^{-1};

for some
p_{1},p_{2}\in[m]
and
i_{*},j_{*}\in[n]
. Assumption
5.2
(3) implies that whenever
\tau_{i_{*}}^{-1}<\tau_{j_{*}}^{-1}
,
z_{1}<y_{1}
.

Let
T_{1,j_{*}}
(resp.
T_{2,j_{*}}
) be the subset of
\mathcal{R}_{j,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{j,2}
(resp.
\mathcal{R}_{j,2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{j,1}
) corresponding to
\tau_{j_{*}}
. Let

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{q}

be all the points in
T_{1,j_{*}}
and let

\displaystyle\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<\ldots<\beta_{q}

be all the points in
T_{2,j_{*}}
. Then one can see that

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}<\beta_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\beta_{2}\ldots<\alpha_{q}<\beta_{q}.

Using similar arguments for
j=2,\ldots,8
, one can verify the remaining part of Assumption
5.1
.
∎

By (
2.12
), for
\alpha>0
integration by parts we obtain

(5.3)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-n\alpha\kappa}\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}d\kappa=\frac{1}{n\alpha\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\right]^{\alpha}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\alpha}\frac{dw}{w}.

Let
\mathbf{m}_{\chi}
be the measure on
(0,\infty)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)=\left.e^{-\kappa}\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}|d\kappa|\right|_{\kappa=-\ln s}

Note that the density of
\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)
with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
is given by

(5.4)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)}{ds}=\left.\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}\right|_{\kappa=-\ln s};

which is exactly the slope of the limiting rescaled height function in the
\kappa
-direction when
s=e^{-\kappa}
.

By (
5.3
)
we deduce that for any
\chi\in(l^{(0)},r^{(0)})

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)<\infty
, i.e.
\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)
is a measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
with finite total mass. Note also that for any positive integer
j
, by (
5.3
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{j-1}\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\kappa j}\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}d\kappa

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{j\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}\leq C^{j+1}

where
C>0
is a positive constant independent of
j
Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{2C}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)\leq\frac{\int_{2C}^{\infty}s^{j-1}\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)}{(2C)^{j-1}}\leq\frac{1}{4C^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{j+1}\longrightarrow 0

as
j\rightarrow\infty
. Hence we obtain that
\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)
has compact support in
(0,\infty)
.

We now compute the density of the measure
\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)
with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Recall that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)}{ds}=-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\pi}\Im\left(\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)\right)

where
\Im
denotes the imaginary part of a complex number and
\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}
is the Stieltjes transform of the measure
\mathbf{m}_{\chi}
, which can be computed as follows:
for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{m}_{\chi})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\zeta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)}{\zeta-s}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{i}\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)}{\zeta^{i+1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-j\kappa}\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}d\kappa

Again by (
5.3
) we obtain

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\zeta)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}j\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}.

When the contour
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the the conditions given as in Proposition
4.5
, under Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
, we can write each integral into the sum of the residue at 0 and the residues at all the points in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{\chi}}
. We can compute the residue at 0 as follows:

\displaystyle S_{0}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{2}{\zeta^{j}j}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}

\displaystyle=-2\prod_{k\geq 1}\log\left(1-\frac{[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)]^{\frac{1}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\right]^{\frac{1}{n}}}{\zeta}\right).

where
\left|\frac{[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)]^{\frac{1}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\right]^{\frac{1}{n}}}{\zeta}\right|<1
to ensure the convergence of the Maclaurin series.

Lemma 5.5
.

The part of (
5.5
) corresponding to the sum of residues at all the points of
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
can be computed by

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\log\left(1-\frac{[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{1}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}}{\zeta}\right)\frac{dw}{w}

where

\displaystyle\sup_{K}\max_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\left|\frac{[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{1}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\frac{1}{n}}}{\zeta}\right|<1

to ensure the convergence of Maclaurin series;
\mathcal{C}_{1,K}
is a simple closed curve enclosing all the poles of
\left[\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\right]\mathcal{G}_{1,>\chi}(w)\mathcal{G}_{0,>\chi}(w)
, but no other poles of the integrand with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w):

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{1,>V_{0}}(u^{-2k}v^{-2k}w)\mathcal{G}_{0,>V_{0}}(u^{-2k}v^{-2k}w)\mathcal{G}_{1,>V_{0}}(u^{2-2k}v^{-2k}w)\mathcal{G}_{0,>V_{0}}(u^{2-2k}v^{-2k}w);

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(2)}(w):

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{1,<V_{m}}(u^{2k}v^{2k}w)\mathcal{G}_{0,<V_{m}}(u^{2k}v^{2k}w)\mathcal{G}_{1,<V_{m}}(u^{2k}v^{2k-2}w)\mathcal{G}_{0,<V_{m}}(u^{2k}v^{2k-2}w).

Then the part of (
5.5
) corresponding to the sum of residues at all the points of
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
is

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{D}_{\chi}}:=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}j\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(2)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}\right]-S_{0}

Under Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
,
\mathcal{C}
is a simple closed curve enclosing all the poles of
\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\mathcal{G}_{1,>\chi}(w)\mathcal{G}_{0,>\chi}(w)
and 0, but no other poles of the integrand. By the bounded convergence theorem we obtain that

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{D}_{\chi}}:=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}j\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(2)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}\right]-S_{0}

Since
\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}
has only finitely many poles, we obtain

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{D}_{\chi}}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}j\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}I_{K},

where

\displaystyle I_{K}:=\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(2)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}

As
k\rightarrow\infty
, the poles of
\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)
converge to 0 exponentially fast, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|I_{K}-\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}I_{K}\right|\leq CKj\alpha^{Kj}

where
C>0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
are constants independent of
K
and
j
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{D}_{\chi}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}j\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(2)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}^{(1)}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}

\displaystyle=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\prod_{k\geq 1}\frac{1}{1-u^{k}v^{k}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{j}j\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{j}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\frac{j}{n}}\frac{dw}{w}\right]

Then the lemma follows.
∎

Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[-2\log\left(1-\frac{[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)]^{\frac{1}{n}}\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\right]^{\frac{1}{n}}}{\zeta}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\log\left(1-\frac{[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)]^{\frac{1}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\right]^{\frac{1}{n}}}{\zeta}\right)\frac{dw}{w}\right]

We would like to get rid of the fractal exponent for the simplicity of computing complex integrals. To that end, we define another function

(5.6)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\chi}(\zeta):

\displaystyle=\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[-2\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)\frac{dw}{w}\right]

Let
\omega=e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{n}}
, then it is straightforward to check that

\Theta_{\chi}(\zeta)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\omega^{-i}\zeta)
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\Im\Theta_{\chi}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\Im\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\omega^{-i}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon))

Since
\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\zeta)
is continuous in
\zeta
when
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{m}_{\chi})
,
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{m}_{\chi})\in(0,\infty)
, and
\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\overline{\zeta})=\overline{\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\zeta)}
, we obtain that when
s\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{m}_{\chi})
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\Im\Theta_{\chi}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\Im\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(\omega^{-i}s)+\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\Im\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\Im\mathrm{St}_{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)=-\frac{\mathbf{m}_{\chi}(ds)}{ds}

Hence by (
5.4
), to compute the slope of the limiting rescaled height function in the
\kappa
-direction, it suffices to compute

-\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\Im\Theta_{\chi}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)
when
s=e^{-\kappa}
.

By (
5.6
) we obtain

(5.8)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\chi}(\zeta):

\displaystyle=-2\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)d\log w

\displaystyle=-2\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}d\left[\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in K}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)\log w\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\frac{\log w}{\zeta^{n}-\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in K}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}\frac{d[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in K}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]}{dw}dw

To compute the contour integral above, we need to consider the root of the following equation in
w
:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=\zeta^{n}.

Recall that
\mathcal{C}_{1,K}
is the union of positively oriented simple closed curves enclosing every poles of
\mathcal{G}_{1,>\chi}(w)\mathcal{G}_{0,>\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{u,v,k}(w)
; but no other zeros or poles of the integrand. Let
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
be the set consisting of all the poles of
\mathcal{G}_{1,>\chi}(w)\mathcal{G}_{0,>\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{u,v,k}(w)
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
is finite, we may decompose

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1,K}=\cup_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\mathcal{C}_{\xi};

where
\mathcal{C}_{\xi}
is a positively oriented simple closed curve enclosing
\xi
but no other zeros or poles of the integrand.

When
\zeta\rightarrow\infty
, zeros of (
5.9
) will approach poles of
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}
. For each
\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
, let
w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta)
be the root of (
5.9
) such that
\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta)=\xi
.

When
|\zeta|
is sufficiently large,
w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta)
is enclosed by
\mathcal{C}_{\xi}
. Enclosed by each
\mathcal{C}_{\xi}
, there is exactly one zero and one pole for
1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}{\zeta^{n}}
, hence

(5.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}d\left[\log\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}{\zeta^{n}}\right)\log w\right]=0.

By computing residues at each
w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta)
and
\xi
, we obtain

(5.11)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{1,K}}\frac{\log w}{\zeta^{n}-\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}\frac{d[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]}{dw}dw

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left[\log w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta)-\log\xi\right].

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or both Assumption
5.2
and Assumption
5.3
hold. Then for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the equation in
w

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]=s^{n},

has at most one pair of complex conjugate roots.

Proof.

The lemma follows from the same argument as the proof of Lemma 6.5 in
[
32
]
. The idea is that the roots of (
5.12
) are roots of a polynomial, hence the total number of roots (counting multiplicities) is the degree
d_{K}
of the polynomial. The interlacing assumptions of
5.1
or
5.2
and
5.3
guaranteed the existence of at least
d_{K}-2
real roots.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose Assumption
2.6
and (
4.6
) hold. Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
hold.
Assume that equation (
5.12
) in
w
with
s=e^{-\kappa}
has exactly one pair of nonreal conjugate roots and
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}\neq\emptyset
.

(1)

If
[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,2}]\cup[\cup_{k\in[K]}\left([\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k-1,k}]\right)]=\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)<e^{-n\kappa}.

(2)

If
[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,2}][\cup_{k\in[K]}\left([\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k-1,k}]\right)=\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-n\kappa}.

Proof.

Same arguments as the proof of Lemma 6.6 in
[
32
]
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose Assumption
2.6
and (
4.6
) hold. Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or both Assumption
5.2
and Assumption
5.3
hold. Let
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)
be the limit of the rescaled height function of free boundary dimer coverings on rail yard graphs as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, as obtained in Lemma
2.7
. Assume that equation (
5.12
) in
w
with
s=e^{-\kappa}
has exactly one pair of nonreal conjugate roots and
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\neq e^{-n\kappa}
.
Then

(5.13)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}=2-\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\frac{2\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{w}_{+,K})}{\pi}

where
\mathbf{w}_{+,K}
is the unique nonreal root of
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=e^{-n\kappa}
in the upper half plane, and the branch of
\mathrm{arg}(\cdot)
is chosen such that
\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{w}_{+,K})\in(0,\pi)
.

Proof.

By (
5.4
) (
5.7
)(
5.8
), (
5.10
), (
5.11
), we obtain

(5.14)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}

\displaystyle=-\left.\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\pi}\Im\Theta_{\chi}(s+\mathbf{i}\epsilon)\right|_{s=e^{-\kappa}}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-n\kappa}}-\frac{2}{\pi}\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left[\mathrm{arg}(w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa}))-\mathrm{arg}(\xi)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\left(2\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-n\kappa}}-\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left[\mathrm{arg}(w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa}))-\mathrm{arg}(\xi)\right]\right)

where the branch of
\mathrm{arg}
is chosen to have range
(-\pi,\pi]
.
Since each
\xi
is a nonzero real number, we have

\mathrm{arg}(\xi)=\mathbf{1}_{\xi<0}
.

Under the assumption that
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\neq e^{-n\kappa}
, we obtain that
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)\neq e^{-n\kappa}
for all sufficiently large
K
.
The following cases might occur:

(1)

If

\displaystyle[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,2}]\cup

\displaystyle[\cup_{k\geq 1}\left([\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k-1,k}]\right)\neq\emptyset;

then for all sufficiently large
K
,

\displaystyle A_{K}:=[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,2}]\cup

\displaystyle[\cup_{k\in[K]}\left([\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k-1,k}]\right)\neq\emptyset;

In this case
the number of negative poles in
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
is the cardinality
|A_{K}|
of the set
A_{K}
above. Under Assumption
5.1
or Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
, the range of the function
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)
between any two consecutive points in
A_{K}
is
(-\infty,\infty)
, hence there are at least
|A_{K}|-1
negative real roots in
\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa})\}_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}
.
Let

\displaystyle c_{7,K}:=\max A_{1,K}.

(a)

If

\displaystyle E:=[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,2}]\cup

\displaystyle[\cup_{k\geq 1}\left([\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k-1,k}]\right)\neq\emptyset

Then for all sufficiently large
K
,

\displaystyle E_{K}:=[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,2}]\cup

\displaystyle[\cup_{k\in[K]}\left([\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k-1,k}]\right)\neq\emptyset

let

\displaystyle c_{8,K}:=\min E_{K}>0.

(b)

If
E=\emptyset

and

\displaystyle J:=[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,2}]\cup

\displaystyle[\cup_{k\geq 1}\left([\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k-1,k}]\right)\neq\emptyset

Then for all sufficiently large
K
,

\displaystyle J_{K}:=[\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,2}]\cup

\displaystyle[\cup_{k\in[K]}\left([\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k,k}]\cup[\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k-1,k}]\right)\neq\emptyset.

Let

\displaystyle C_{8,K}=\min J_{K}>0.

(c)

If
E=J=\emptyset
, let

\displaystyle C_{8,K}=+\infty.

Then by (
4.7
)-(
4.10
),
the following cases might occur

(a)

\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-\kappa}
: then there exists a unique root in
\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa})\}_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\cap(B_{1},B_{2})
which is negative; in this case the argument of each negative pole in
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
cancels with an argument of a unique negative root in
\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa})\}_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}
;

(b)

\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)<e^{-\kappa}
: then there exists no root in
\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa})\}_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\cap(B_{1},0)
; in this case there is a unique negative pole in
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
whose argument cannot cancel with an argument of a unique negative root in
\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa})\}_{\xi\in\mathcal{R}_{\chi}}
;

in either case we have

\displaystyle 2\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-n\kappa}}-\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left[\mathrm{arg}(w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa}))-\mathrm{arg}(\xi)\right]=2-\frac{2\arg(\mathbf{w}_{+,K})}{\pi}

Then (
5.18
) holds.

(2)

E\neq\emptyset
and

A=\emptyset
. In this case
A_{K}=\emptyset
for any positive integer
K
. There are neither negative poles in
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}
nor negative real roots in
\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(e^{-\kappa})\}_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}
.
By Lemma
5.7
, we have
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-n\kappa}
. Then (
5.18
) follows from (
5.14
).

∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or both Assumption
5.2
and Assumption
5.3
hold. Then for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the equation in
w

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]=s^{n},

has at most one pair of complex conjugate roots. Moreover, if
\mathbf{w}_{+}
be the unique root of (
5.15
) in the upper half plane; then

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{+}=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{w}_{+,K}

where
\mathbf{w}_{+,K}
is given as in Proposition
5.8
.

Proof.

Note that as
K\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in K}[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)]

uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
that do not intersect
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that (
5.15
) has more than one pair of complex conjugate roots; Then by the Hurwitz Theorem (see Lemma
A.8
), when
K
is sufficiently large, then equation (
5.12
) has more than one pair of complex conjugate roots. But this is a contradiction to Lemma
5.6
. The convergence (
5.16
) again follows from Lemmas
A.8
and
5.6
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.
By Lemma
A.8
, from (
5.14
) we obtain
By (
5.4
) (
5.7
)(
5.8
), (
5.10
), (
5.11
), we obtain

(5.17)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}=2\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(0)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(0)>e^{-n\kappa}}-\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi}}\left[\mathrm{arg}(w_{\xi,\chi}(e^{-\kappa}))-\mathrm{arg}(\xi)\right]

where
w_{\xi,\chi}=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}w_{\xi,\chi,K}
is a root of the 1st equation of (
2.13
) which approaches the pole
\xi
when
\kappa\rightarrow-\infty
.
It follows that when the first equation of (
2.13
) has only real roots,
(\chi,\kappa)
is always in the frozen region.

When the first equation of (
2.13
) has a unique pair of nonreal conjugate roots, by Lemma
5.9
and (
5.18
) we obtain

(5.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}=2-\frac{2\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{w}_{+})}{\pi}.

where
\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{w}_{+})\in(0,\pi)
, hence
(\chi,\kappa)
is in the liquid region in this case.

Then the frozen boundary is given by the condition that the first equation of (
2.13
) has double real roots, which gives the system of equations (
2.13
).

\Box

See (
A.12
) for the expression of the second equation in (
2.13
).

6.
Height Fluctuations and Gaussian Free Field

In Section
6
, we prove that the fluctuations of unrescaled height function converges to the pull-back of the restriction Gaussian Free Field (GFF) in the upper half plane to a subset under a diffeomorphism from the liquid region to the subset of the upper half plane. The main theorem proved in this section is Theorem
2.10
.

6.1.
Gaussian free field

Let
C_{0}^{\infty}
be the space of smooth real-valued functions with compact
support in the upper half plane
{\mathbb{H}}
. The
Gaussian free field
(GFF)

\Xi
on
{\mathbb{H}}
with the zero boundary condition is a collection of Gaussian
random variables
\{\Xi_{f}\}_{f\in C_{0}^{\infty}}
indexed by functions in

C_{0}^{\infty}
, such that the covariance of two Gaussian random variables

\Xi_{f_{1}}
,
\Xi_{f_{2}}
is given by

(6.1)

\mathrm{Cov}(\Xi_{f_{1}},\Xi_{f_{2}})=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}f_{1}(z)f_{2}(w)G_{{\mathbb{H}}}(z,w)dzd\overline{z}dwd\overline{w},

where

G_{{\mathbb{H}}}(z,w):=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\left|\frac{z-w}{z-\overline{w}}\right|,\qquad z,w\in{\mathbb{H}}

is the Green’s function of the Laplacian operator on
{\mathbb{H}}
with the Dirichlet
boundary condition. The Gaussian free field
\Xi
can also be considered as a
random distribution on
C_{0}^{\infty}
,
such that for any
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

\Xi(f)=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}f(z)\Xi(z)dz:=\xi_{f}.

Here
\Xi(f)
is the Gaussian random variable with respect to
f
, which has
mean 0 and variance given by (
6.1
)) with
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
replaced by
f
.
See
[
38
]
for more about the GFF.

6.2.
\mathbf{w}_{+}
as a mapping from
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathbb{H}

:

By (
4.7
)-(
4.10
) and (
4.12
)-(
4.13
), we may write
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)
as the quotient of two functions
U_{\chi}(w)
and
R(w)
, such that
U_{\chi}(w)
depends on
\chi
and
R(w)
is independent of
\chi
. More precisely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=\frac{U_{\chi}(w)}{R(w)};

where

\displaystyle U_{\chi}(w)=e^{\phi(\chi)}\frac{\prod_{j\in[n],a_{j}=R}\left(1+e^{-\chi}w\tau_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j\in[n],a_{j}=L}({1-e^{-\chi}w\tau_{j}})}

and

\displaystyle\phi(\chi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in[m],V_{p-1}>\chi}\sum_{j\in[n],(a_{j},b_{j}(p-1,p))=(L,-)}(V_{p}-V_{p-1})+\sum_{p\in[m],V_{p-1}<\chi<V_{p}}\sum_{j\in[n],(a_{j},b_{j}(p-1,p))=(L,-)}(V_{p}-\chi)

\displaystyle-\sum_{p\in[m],V_{p-1}>\chi}\sum_{j\in[n],(a_{j},b_{j}(p-1,p))=(R,-)}(V_{p}-V_{p-1})-\sum_{p\in[m],V_{p-1}<\chi<V_{p}}\sum_{j\in[n],(a_{j},b_{j}(p-1,p))=(R,-)}(V_{p}-\chi)

(6.2)

\displaystyle R(w)=\frac{A_{1}\cdot A_{2}}{\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)}

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p}>\chi)}\left(1-e^{-V_{p}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,>,1}}}\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p-1}>\chi)}\left(1-we^{-V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,>,1}}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p-1}<\chi)}\left(1-we^{-V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,<,1}}}\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p}<\chi)}\left(1-e^{-V_{p}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,<,1}}}

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p}>\chi)}\left(1+e^{-V_{p}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,>,0}}}\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p-1}>\chi)}\left({1+we^{-V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}}\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,>,0}}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p}<\chi)}\left(1+e^{-V_{p}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,<,0}}}\prod_{(p\in[m],V_{p-1}<\chi)}\left({1+we^{-V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,<,0}}}

For
j\in[n]
and
p\in[m]
we use
b_{j}(p-1,p)=+
to denote that for each
\epsilon>0
, and each integer
k
such that
\epsilon k\in\left(V_{p-1},V_{p}\right)
and
[(k-j)\mod n]=0
,
b_{k}^{(\epsilon)}=+
. Then

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in[n]:a_{j}=L}\left[\left(1-e^{-V_{0}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(0,1)=+}}\left(1-e^{-V_{m}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(m-1,m)=-}}\right]

(6.3)

\displaystyle\times\left[\prod_{p=1}^{m-1}\left(1-e^{-V_{p}}\tau_{j}w\right)^{-\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(p,p+1)=+}+\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(p-1,p)=+}}\right]

and

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in[n]:a_{j}=R}\left[\left(1+e^{-V_{0}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(0,1)=+}}\left(1+e^{-V_{m}}w\tau_{j}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(m-1,m)=-}}\right]

(6.4)

\displaystyle\times\left[\prod_{p=1}^{m-1}\left(1+e^{-V_{p}}\tau_{j}w\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(p,p+1)=+}-\mathbf{1}_{b_{j}(p-1,p)=+}}\right]

We shall always use
[\cdot]^{\frac{1}{n}}
to denote the branch which takes positive real values on the positive real line.
Define

\displaystyle U(w_{*},z_{*})=\left[\frac{\prod_{j\in[n],a_{j}=R}\left(1+w_{*}\tau_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j\in[n],a_{j}=L}({1-w_{*}\tau_{j}})}\right]^{\frac{1}{n}}-z_{*}

Hence we have

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=e^{-n\kappa}

if and only if

(6.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}U(w_{*},z_{*})=0\\
[R(w)]^{\frac{1}{n}}=z\\
(w_{*},z_{*})=(e^{-\chi}w,e^{-\kappa-\frac{\phi(\chi)}{n}}z)\end{cases}

Lemma 6.1
.

Let

\displaystyle n_{R}:=|\{j\in[n]:a_{j}=R\}|;\qquad n_{L}:=|\{j\in[n]:a_{j}=L\}|;

For any
(\alpha,\theta)
such that

(6.7)

\displaystyle\theta\in(0,\pi),\qquad\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{n_{L}\pi+(n_{R}-n_{L})\theta}{n}\right),

there exists a unique pair
(w_{*},z_{*})
such that
\arg z_{*}=\alpha
,
\arg w_{*}=\theta
and
U(w_{*},z_{*})=0
.

Proof.

See Lemma 7.1 of
[
32
]
.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

For each
(\chi,\kappa)\in\mathcal{L}
, let
\mathbf{w}_{+}(\chi,\kappa)
be the unique root of (
6.5
) in the upper half plane
{\mathbb{H}}
. Then
\mathbf{w}_{+}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathbf{w}_{+}(\mathcal{L})\subset{\mathbb{H}}
is a diffeomorphism.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathbf{w}_{+}
is a bijection between
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathbf{w}_{+}(\mathcal{L})
.
For any
w\in\mathbf{w}_{+}(\mathcal{L})\subset{\mathbb{H}}
, let
z=[R(w)]^{\frac{1}{n}}
. Let

\displaystyle\theta:=\arg w\in(0,\pi);\qquad\alpha:=\arg z

By Lemma
6.1
, if

\displaystyle\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{n_{L}\pi+(n_{R}-n_{L})\theta}{n}\right)

there exists a unique pair
(w_{*},z_{*})
, such that
\arg z_{*}=\alpha
,
\arg w_{*}=\theta
and
U(w_{*},z_{*})=0
.

Let

\displaystyle\chi:=\log\left(\frac{w}{w_{*}}\right);\qquad\kappa:=\log\left(\frac{z}{z_{*}}\right)-\frac{\phi(\chi)}{n}

where the branch of the
\log(\cdot)
is chosen such that it takes real values on the positive real axis. Then we deduce that
\mathbf{w}_{+}
is a bijection. From the process we see that both the mapping
\mathbf{w}_{+}
and its inverse are differentiable. Then the proposition follows.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or both Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
hold. Let
K\geq 1
. One of the following two conditions holds:

(1)

(6.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=+\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{6}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=+\infty;

(6.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{8}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=-\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=-\infty.

(6.10)

\displaystyle\infty>\min_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)>\max_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)>-\infty.

(2)

(6.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=-\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{6}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=-\infty;

(6.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{8}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=+\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow c_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=+\infty.

(6.13)

\displaystyle\infty>\min_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)>\max_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)>-\infty.

where
c_{3}=c_{3,K}
,
c_{2}=c_{2,K}
for all
K
,
c_{3,K}
,
c_{2},K
,
c_{6}
,
c_{5}
are given as in Assumption
5.1
.

Proof.

Note that under Assumption
5.1
, we may write

(6.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=C_{K}\frac{B_{K}(w)}{T_{K}(w)}

where

\displaystyle\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}C_{K}=C>0

and

\displaystyle B_{K}(w)=\prod_{f_{i,K}\in D_{2,K}\cup D_{4,K}}(w-f_{i,K})

\displaystyle T_{K}(w)=\prod_{g_{i,K}\in D_{1,K}\cup D_{3,K}}(w-g_{i,K})

For
i\in[4]
, let
D_{i,K}
be given as in Assumption
5.1
. We can decompose (
6.14
) into partial fractions as follows:

\displaystyle(\ref{qgf})=C_{K}\left(1+\sum_{g_{i,K}\in D_{1,K}\cup D_{3,K}}\frac{1}{w-g_{i,K}}\frac{B_{K}(g_{i,K})}{T_{k}^{\prime}(g_{i,K})}\right)

Under Assumption
5.1
, we obtain

•

It is straightforward to check that for each
g_{i,k}\in D_{1,K}\cup D_{3,K}
, either

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K+}}(\ref{qgf})=-\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i+1,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=+\infty.

or

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K+}}(\ref{qgf})=+\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i+1,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=-\infty.

•

If one of the following two conditions holds

(1)

g_{i,K}<g_{i+1,K}
are two consecutive points in
D_{1,K}
; or

(2)

g_{i,K}<g_{i+1,K}
are two consecutive points in
D_{3,K}
.

In case (1), there are exactly one point in
D_{2,K}
and no point in
D_{4,K}
between
g_{i,K}
and
g_{i+1,K}
; in case (2), there are exactly one point in
D_{4,K}
and no point in
D_{2,K}
between
g_{i,K}
and
g_{i+1,K}
; hence in either case we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{sgn}\left(\frac{B_{K}(g_{i,K}+)}{T_{K}^{\prime}(g_{i,K}+)}\right)=-\mathrm{sgn}\left(\frac{B_{K}(g_{i+1,K}-)}{T_{K}^{\prime}(g_{i+1,K}-)}\right)

Therefore either

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=-\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=+\infty.

or

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=+\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=-\infty.

•

If
g_{i,K}<g_{i+1,K}
are two consecutive points in
D_{1,K}\cup D_{3,K}
; and one of the following two conditions holds

(1)

g_{i,K}\in D_{1,K}
and
g_{i+1,K}\in D_{3,K}
;

(2)

g_{i,K}\in D_{3,K}
and
g_{i+1,K}\in D_{1,K}
;

In case (1), there are exactly one point in
D_{2,K}
and one point in
D_{4,K}
between
g_{i,K}
and
g_{i+1,K}
; in case (2), there are exactly no point in
D_{4,K}
and no point in
D_{2,K}
between
g_{i,K}
and
g_{i+1,K}
; hence in either case we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{sgn}\left(\frac{B_{K}(g_{i,K}+)}{T_{K}^{\prime}(g_{i,K}+)}\right)=\mathrm{sgn}\left(\frac{B_{K}(g_{i+1,K}-)}{T_{K}^{\prime}(g_{i+1,K}-)}\right)

Therefore either

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=-\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=-\infty.

or

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=+\infty;\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow g_{i,K-}}(\ref{qgf})=+\infty.

Then (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) or (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) follows.

We now show that if (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) hold, then (
6.10
) holds. We can show that if (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) hold, then (
6.13
) holds similarly.

Suppose that (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) hold, and

\displaystyle\min_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)\leq\max_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

then we can find

\displaystyle s\in\left[\min_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x),\max_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)\right]

such that the number of real roots of the equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=s

is at least
|D_{1,k}\cup D_{3,K}|+2
(counting multiplicities), which is a contradiction to the fact that the equation has exactly
|D_{1,k}\cup D_{3,K}|
roots in the complex plane (counting multiplicities). Then the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or both Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
hold. Let
\chi\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

If case (1) in Lemma
6.3
holds, let

\displaystyle x_{m,K}=\mathrm{argmax}_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

\displaystyle x_{M,K}=\mathrm{argmin}_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

\displaystyle m_{K}=\mathrm{max}_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

\displaystyle M_{K}=\mathrm{min}_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

•

If case (2) in Lemma
6.3
holds, let

\displaystyle x_{m,K}=\mathrm{argmax}_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

\displaystyle x_{M,K}=\mathrm{argmin}_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

\displaystyle m_{k}=\mathrm{max}_{x\in(c_{3},c_{6})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

\displaystyle M_{K}=\mathrm{argmin}_{x\in(c_{8},c_{2})}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)

Then both
x_{m,K}
and
x_{M,K}
are well defined and unique. Moreover,
x_{m,K}
is the unique double real root of

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=m_{K}

and
x_{M,K}
is the unique double real root of

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=M_{K}

and for any
s\in(-\infty,m_{K})\cup(M_{k},\infty)
, the equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(x)\prod_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(x)=s

has no non-real roots.

Proof.

The lemma follows from the proof of Lemma
6.3
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that either Assumption
5.1
holds or both Assumptions
5.2
and
5.3
hold. Consider the vertical line
\chi=\chi_{0}
. Let
\kappa_{0,m}
(resp.
\kappa_{0,M}
) be the minimal (resp. maximal) ordinate of points in the intersection of the frozen boundary with
\chi=\chi_{0}
. Let
x_{m}
be the unique nonzero double real root of

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi_{0}}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=e^{-n\kappa_{0,m}}

and let
x_{M}
be the unique nonzero double real root of

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi_{0}}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)=e^{-n\kappa_{0,M}}

then one of the following two conditions hold.

•

x_{m}\in(c_{3},c_{6})
, and

; or

x_{m}\in(c_{8},c_{2})
, and

by letting

.

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

Same arguments as the proof of Theorem 7.7 in
[

]
.

\Box

7.
Sampling Algorithm

Now we describe an algorithm to sample double-free boundary dimer coverings on rail-yard graphs. Algorithms to sample one free boundary, one empty boundary Schur processes can be found in
[
3
]
. The idea is based on repeatedly commuting and reflecting the Bosonic operators with respect to the
left and right boundary, inspired by the computation of partition functions of double-sided free boundary steep tilings in
[
10
]
. Since the partition function includes an infinite product, the sampling algorithm here to sample the double-sided free boundary Schur processes is not an exact sampling, but the sample obtained by this algorithm has the distribution an approximation of the Gibbs measure, if we run the algorithm for sufficiently many steps.

First we review a few algorithms in
[
3
]
. The goal of algorithm
7.1
is, given 3 partitions
\lambda,\mu,\kappa\in{\mathbb{Y}}
and a geometric random variable
G\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
\kappa\prec\lambda
and
\kappa\prec\mu
, sample a partition
\nu
such that
\mu\prec\nu
,
\lambda\prec\nu
,
|\nu|+|\kappa|=|\lambda|+|\mu|+G
, and for fixed
(\lambda,\mu)
, the mapping
(\kappa,G)\rightarrow\nu
is a bijection.

Algorithm 7.1
.

def
HH(\lambda,\mu,\kappa,\xi)

•

sample
G\sim\mathrm{Geom}(\xi)
;

•

construct a partition
\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\nu_{i}=\begin{cases}\max\{\lambda_{1},\mu_{1}\}+G&\mathrm{if}\ i=1\\
\max\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}+\min\{\lambda_{i-1},\mu_{i-1}\}-\kappa_{i-1}&\mathrm{if}\ i>1.\end{cases}

•

return
\nu
.

Here
\mathrm{Geom}(\xi)
is a geometric random variable such that
P(\mathrm{Geom}(\xi)=k)=\xi^{k}(1-\xi)
for
k\geq 0
.

The goal of algorithm
7.2
is, given 3 partitions
\lambda,\mu,\kappa\in{\mathbb{Y}}
and a Bernoulli random variable
B\in\{0,1\}
satisfying
\kappa\prec^{\prime}\lambda
and
\kappa\prec\mu
, sample a partition
\nu
such that
\mu\prec^{\prime}\nu
,
\lambda\prec\nu
,
|\nu|+|\kappa|=|\lambda|+|\mu|+B
, and for fixed
(\lambda,\mu)
, the mapping
(\kappa,B)\rightarrow\nu
is a bijection.

Algorithm 7.2
.

def
HV(\lambda,\mu,\kappa,\xi)

•

sample
B\sim\mathrm{Bern}\left(\frac{\xi}{1+\xi}\right)
;

•

for
i=1\ldots\max\{l(\lambda),l(\mu)\}+1
,

–

if
\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i}<\lambda_{i-1}
, then
\nu_{i}=\max\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}+B
, else
\nu_{i}=\max\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}
;

–

if
\mu_{i+1}<\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i}
, then
B=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}-\kappa_{i}
.

•

return
\nu
.

Here
\mathrm{Bern}(p)
is a Bernoulli random variable taking value 1 with probability
p
.

Besides Algorithms
7.1
and
7.2
, we now introduce two other algorithms.

The goal of algorithm
7.3
is, given 3 partitions
\lambda,\mu,\kappa\in{\mathbb{Y}}
satisfying
\mu\prec\kappa\prec\lambda
, sample a partition
\nu
such that
\mu\prec\nu\prec\lambda
,
|\nu|+|\kappa|=|\lambda|+|\mu|
, and for fixed
(\lambda,\mu)
, the mapping
\kappa\rightarrow\nu
is a bijection.

Algorithm 7.3
.

def
AA(\lambda,\mu,\kappa)

•

construct a partition
\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
as follows:
\nu_{i}=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i-1}\}+\max\{\lambda_{i+1},\mu_{i}\}-\kappa_{i}
.

•

return
\nu
.

The goal of algorithm
7.4
is, given 3 partitions
\lambda,\mu,\kappa\in{\mathbb{Y}}
satisfying
\mu\prec^{\prime}\kappa\prec\lambda
, sample a partition
\nu
such that
\mu\prec\nu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
,
|\nu|+|\kappa|=|\lambda|+|\mu|
, and for fixed
(\lambda,\mu)
, the mapping
\kappa\rightarrow\nu
is a bijection.

Algorithm 7.4
.

def
AB(\lambda,\mu,\kappa)

•

for
1\leq i\leq\max\{l(\lambda),l(\mu)\}+1
;

–

If
\lambda_{i+1}\leq\mu_{i}<\lambda_{i}
,

\delta=\kappa_{i}-\max\{\lambda_{i+1},\mu_{i}\}
; else
\delta=0
.

–

if
\mu_{i}<\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i-1}
,
\nu_{i}=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i-1}\}-\delta
; else,
\nu_{i}=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i-1}\}
.

•

return
\nu
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Given 3 partitions
\lambda,\mu,\kappa\in{\mathbb{Y}}
satisfying
\mu\prec^{\prime}\kappa\prec\lambda
, Algorithm
7.4
returns a partition
\nu
such that
\mu\prec\nu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
,
|\nu|+|\kappa|=|\lambda|+|\mu|
, and for fixed
(\lambda,\mu)
, the mapping
\kappa\rightarrow\nu
is a bijection.

Proof.

Note that the condition
\mu\prec^{\prime}\kappa\prec\lambda
gives

\displaystyle\max\{\lambda_{i+1},\mu_{i}\}\leq\kappa_{i}\leq\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}+1\}.

Then condition
\mu\prec\nu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
gives

(7.1)

\displaystyle\max\{\lambda_{i}-1,\mu_{i}\}\leq\nu_{i}\leq\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i-1}\}.

Hence we obtain that
\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i-1}\}-\nu_{i}=0
unless

(7.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{i}<\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i-1},

in which case
\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i-1}\}-\nu_{i}
may take the value 1 as well. Let
i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{r}
be the
i
’s such that (
7.2
) holds. Similarly
\kappa_{i}-\max\{\lambda_{i+1},\mu_{i}\}=0
unless

(7.3)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i+1}\leq\mu_{i}<\lambda_{i}

in which case
\kappa_{i}-\max\{\lambda_{i+1},\mu_{i}\}=0
may take the value 1 as well. Let
j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{s}
be the
i
’s such that (
7.3
) holds. We claim that
s=r
and

\displaystyle j_{1}<i_{1}\leq j_{2}<i_{2}\ldots\leq j_{r}<i_{r}.

Indeed, the mapping
\gamma
defined by

\displaystyle\gamma(j):=\min\{i>j:\mu_{i}<\lambda_{i}\}

gives a bijection from
\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\}
to
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
. Then it follows that
|\nu|+|\kappa|=|\lambda|+|\mu|
. (
7.1
), and therefore
\mu\prec\nu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
can also be checked from the construction of
\nu
.
∎

Let
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
be a fixed large positive integer. Let
m_{1},\ldots,m_{K}
be sampled according to independent geometric distributions such that

\displaystyle m_{k}\sim\mathrm{Geom}((uv)^{k}),\ \forall k\in[K].

Then we construct a partition
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
whose maximal part is at most
K
satisfying

\displaystyle m_{k}(\lambda)=m_{k},\ \forall k\in[K]

where
m_{k}(\lambda)
is the number of parts in
\lambda
equal to
k
, as in (
A.5
).

Let

\displaystyle N_{-}:=\{j\in[l..r],b_{j}=-\};\qquad N_{+}:=\{j\in[l..r],b_{j}=+\};

\displaystyle N:=N_{-}+N_{+}.

This also gives an increasing bijection
\psi_{-}
from
[N_{-}]
to
\{j\in[l..r],b_{j}=-\}
, and an increasing bijection
\psi_{+}
from
[N_{+}]
to
\{j\in[l..r],b_{j}=+\}
.

To each point in the set

\displaystyle I:=\{(i,-NK),(-NK,j):i\in[-N_{+}-NK..-NK],j\in[-N_{-}-NK..-NK]\}

associate a partition

\displaystyle\lambda^{(i,j)}=\lambda;\ \forall(i,j)\in I.

Let

\displaystyle s:=-Nr+q+1;\qquad r\in[K],\ q\in[0..N-1]

Assume we have obtained partitions

\displaystyle\lambda^{(i,s-1)},\forall i\in[-N_{+}+s-1..s-1];\ \mathrm{and}\ \lambda^{(s-1,j)},\forall j\in[-N_{-}+s-1..s-1].

for some
s\in[K]
.
Now we sample partitions

\displaystyle\lambda^{(i,s)},\forall i\in[-N_{+}+s..s];\ \mathrm{and}\ \lambda^{(s,j)},\forall j\in[-N_{-}+s..s].

in the following way:

(1)

If
q<N_{-}
:

(a)

When
i
decreases from
-N_{-}+s-1+q
to
-N_{-}+s
,
let
j:=i+N_{-}-s
.
Update the partition
\lambda^{(s-1,i)}
to

\tilde{\lambda}^{(s-1,i)}
as follows:

(i)

Let
\tilde{\lambda}^{(s-1,-N_{-}+s-1+q)}=\lambda^{(s-1,-N_{-}+s-1+q)}
;

(ii)

If
a_{\psi_{-}(N_{-}-q)}=L
and
a_{\psi_{-}(N_{-}-j)}=L
, let

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}^{(s-1,i)}=AA(\tilde{\lambda}^{(s-1,i+1)},\lambda^{(s-1,i-1)},\lambda^{(s-1,i)})

(iii)

If
a_{\psi_{-}(N_{-}-q)}=L
and
a_{\psi_{-}(N_{-}-j)}=R
, let

(3)

(d)

For
t\in[s+N_{-}-q..s-1]
, let
\pi_{t}
be the part of the boundary of of all the integral points
(i,j)
, where
\lambda^{(i,j)}
has been sampled, starting from

, ending in
(s-1,-N_{-}+s-1)
, and winding clockwise.
Let
i_{t}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
be the vertical coordinate of the
(t+N_{+}-s-1)
th unit horizontal segment of
\pi_{t}
and let
z
be the right endpoint of of the
(t+N_{+}-s-1)
th unit horizontal segment of
\pi_{t}
, then following
z
, along
\pi_{t}
there is a vertical edge
zw
.
The following cases might occur:

(i)

If following
w
,
\pi_{t}
has a horizontal unit,
sample the partition
\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t})}
as follows

(A)

If
a_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N_{+}+1)}=a_{\psi_{+}(q+1-N_{-})}=L
, let

\displaystyle\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t})}=HH(\lambda^{(t,i_{t})},\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t}-1)},\lambda^{(t,i_{t}-1)},u^{4r-4}v^{4r-2}x_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N_{+}+1)}x_{\psi_{+}(q+1-N_{-})})

a_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N_{+}+1)}=a_{\psi_{+}(q+1-N_{-})}=R
, let

\displaystyle\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t})}=[HH([\lambda^{(t,i_{t})}]^{\prime},[\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t}-1)}]^{\prime},[\lambda^{(t,i_{t}-1)}]^{\prime},u^{4r-4}v^{4r-2}x_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N_{+}+1)}x_{\psi_{+}(q+1-N_{-})})]^{\prime}

, and

\displaystyle\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t})}=HV(\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t}-1)},\lambda^{(t,i_{t})},\lambda^{(t,i_{t}-1)},u^{4r-4}v^{4r-2}x_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N_{+}+1)}x_{\psi_{+}(q+1-N_{-})})

(D)

a_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N^{+})}=R
, and

\displaystyle\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t})}=[HV([\lambda^{(t+1,i_{t}-1)}]^{\prime},[\lambda^{(t,i_{t})}]^{\prime},[\lambda^{(t,i_{t}-1)}]^{\prime},u^{4r-4}v^{4r-2}x_{\psi_{+}(t-s+N_{+}+1)}x_{\psi_{+}(q+1-N_{-})})]^{\prime}

(F)

continue similar updates in (B) until the next edge along
\pi_{t}
is horizontal. Then we still use
\lambda

\displaystyle\eta:=|\{i\in[l..r]:b_{i}=-,i>\psi_{+}(q+1-N)\}|

For

.

Consider the part
\mathcal{D}
of the boundary of all points
(i,j)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
such that
\lambda^{(i,j)}
has been sampled, starting from
(-N_{+},0)
and ending in
(0,-N_{-})
and winding clockwise, the sequence of partitions
\lambda^{(i,j)}
along all the points of this part of (oriented) boundary gives a sample of sequence of partitions corresponding to double-free-boundary dimer coverings on the rail yard graph.

Theorem 7.6
.

Assume
\mathcal{D}=(z_{0},\ldots,z_{N})
. Then

(1)

the ordered sequence of partitions

(7.4)

\displaystyle(\lambda^{z_{0}},\ldots,\lambda^{z_{N}})

have distribution proportional to the product of weights of present edges in the dimer covering, conditional on

•

\lambda_{1}^{z_{0}}\leq K
and
\lambda_{1}^{z_{N}}\leq K
; and

•

\lambda^{(-N_{+}-NK,-NK)}=\lambda^{(-N_{+}-NK+1,-NK)}=\ldots=\lambda^{(-NK,-NK)}=\lambda^{(-NK,-NK-1)}=\ldots=\lambda^{(-NK,-NK-N_{-})}
;

(2)

as
K\rightarrow\infty
, the distribution of (
7.4
) converges to the Gibbs measure of double-sided free boundary dimer configurations.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check Part (1) of the theorem from the sampling process. For Part (2), note that the partition function of double-sided free boundary conditions that do not satisfy the condition of Part (1) if of order
(uv)^{K}
if the partition function of all the configurations, which tends to 0 as
K\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

8.
Example

Example 8.1
.

Let

\displaystyle V_{0}=0,\ V_{1}=1,\ m=1,\ n=4

and

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1})=(L,-),\ (a_{2},b_{2})=(L,+),\ (a_{3},b_{3})=(R,+),\ (a_{4},b_{4})=(R,-),\

and

\displaystyle\tau_{1}=\tau_{4}=1,\ \tau_{2}=\tau_{3}=0.3,\ u=v=0.1

We have

(8.1)

\displaystyle g_{k}(w):

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w-(0.01)^{2k}e}-\frac{1}{w-(0.01)^{2k}}+\frac{1}{w-(0.1)^{4k-2}e}-\frac{1}{w-(0.1)^{4k-2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{w-(0.01)^{-2k}[0.3]^{-1}}-\frac{1}{w-(0.01)^{-2k}e[0.3]^{-1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{w-(0.1)^{2-4k}[0.3]^{-1}}-\frac{1}{w-(0.1)^{2-4k}e[0.3]^{-1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{w+(0.01)^{2k}}-\frac{1}{w+(0.01)^{2k}e}+\frac{1}{w+(0.1)^{4k-2}}-\frac{1}{w+(0.1)^{4k-2}e}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{w+(0.01)^{-2k}e[0.3]^{-1}}-\frac{1}{w+(0.01)^{-2k}[0.3]^{-1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{w+(0.1)^{2-4k}e[0.3]^{-1}}-\frac{1}{w+(0.1)^{2-4k}[0.3]^{-1}}

The second equation in (
2.13
) gives

(8.2)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w-e}-\frac{1}{w-e^{\chi}}+\frac{1}{w-[0.3]^{-1}}-\frac{1}{w-e^{\chi}[0.3]^{-1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{w+e^{\chi}}-\frac{1}{w+e}+\frac{1}{w+e^{\chi}[0.3]^{-1}}-\frac{1}{w+[0.3]^{-1}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}g_{k}(w)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{w-e}{w-e^{\chi}}\right)\left(\frac{1-0.3w}{1-0.3e^{-\chi}w}\right)\left(\frac{w+e^{\chi}}{w+e}\right)\left(\frac{1+0.3e^{-\chi}w}{1+0.3w}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{w-(0.01)^{2k}e}{w-(0.01)^{2k}}\right)\left(\frac{w-(0.1)^{4k-2}e}{w-(0.1)^{4k-2}}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1-[0.3]w(0.01)^{2k}}{1-[0.3]e^{-1}w(0.01)^{2k}}\right)\left(\frac{1-[0.3]w(0.1)^{4k-2}}{1-[0.3]e^{-1}w(0.1)^{4k-2}}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{w+(0.01)^{2k}}{w+(0.01)^{2k}e}\right)\left(\frac{w+(0.1)^{4k-2}}{w+(0.1)^{4k-2}e}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1+[0.3]e^{-1}(0.01)^{2k}w}{1+[0.3](0.01)^{2k}w}\right)\left(\frac{1+[0.3]e^{-1}(0.1)^{4k-2}w}{1+[0.3](0.1)^{4k-2}w}\right)

See Figure for an approximation of the frozen boundary with
K=10
; See Figure for a simulation of the configurations with
K=3
,
N=70
using the algorithm in Section
8
.

Appendix A
Technical Results

In Appendix
A
we review facts about Macdonald polynomials and include some known technical results used in this paper.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
graded algebra. The topological completion
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined to be the algebra of all formal series

\displaystyle a=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k},\ a_{k}\in\mathcal{A},\mathrm{deg}(a_{k})=k.

For
a\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, the lower degree
\mathrm{ldeg}(a)
of
a
is defined as follows

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ldeg}(a)=\max\{K:a_{k}=0,\ \forall k<K\}

Let
\Lambda_{X}
be the algebra of symmetric functions on a countable set of variables
X=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\ldots)
. A specialization

\displaystyle\rho:\Lambda_{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

is an algebra homomorphism. Define the power sum symmetric function on countable many variables by

(A.2)

\displaystyle p_{n}(X):=\begin{cases}1&\mathrm{if}\ n=0\\
\sum_{x_{i}\in X}x_{i}^{n}&\mathrm{if}\ n\geq 1.\end{cases}

Then
\Lambda_{X}
is generated by these power sum symmetric functions. Hence a specialization
\rho
is uniquely determined by the values
\{\rho(p_{n}(X))\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
; see
[
5
]
.

For symmetric functions
f\in\Lambda_{X}
, we write
f(\rho):=\rho(f)
.
Then a specialization
\rho
is also uniquely determined by its generating function
H(\rho;t)
defined as follows:

\displaystyle H(\rho;t):=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{p_{n}(\rho)t^{n}}{n}\right)

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
be two specializations and
c\in\mathbb{C}
. Define
\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
,
c\rho
,
c*\rho
be new specializations with generating functions given by

\displaystyle H(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2};t):=H(\rho_{1},t)H(\rho_{2},t);\qquad H(c*\rho;t):=[H(\rho,t)]^{c};\qquad

\displaystyle H(c\rho;t):=H(\rho,ct).

respectively. Equivalently for all
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle p_{n}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2})=p_{n}(\rho_{1})+p_{n}(\rho_{2});\qquad

\displaystyle p_{n}(c*\rho)=cp_{n}(\rho);\qquad

\displaystyle p_{n}(c\rho)=c^{n}p_{n}(\rho).

Define

(A.3)

\displaystyle H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}s_{\lambda}(\rho_{1})s_{\lambda}(\rho_{2})=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{p_{n}(\rho_{1})p_{n}(\rho_{2})}{n}\right)

(A.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}(\rho):

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}s_{\lambda}(\rho)=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{p_{2n-1}(\rho)}{2n-1}+\frac{p_{n}(\rho)^{2}}{2n}\right)

In particular, when
\rho_{2}
is the specialization of a single variable
t
,
H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2})=H(\rho_{1},t)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle H(\rho;\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2})=H(\rho;\rho_{1})H(\rho;\rho_{2});\qquad\widetilde{H}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2})=\widetilde{H}(\rho_{1})\widetilde{H}(\rho_{2})H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2}).

For each pair of parameters
q,t\in(0,1)
and each partition
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, define the normalized Macdonald polynomial to be a symmetric polynomial as follows:

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\cdot;q,t)=m_{\lambda}+\sum_{\mu<\lambda}u_{\lambda,\mu}(q,t)m_{\mu}

where
u_{\lambda,\mu}(q,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
m_{\mu}
are the monomial symmetric polynomials; see
[
33
]
. It is known that
\{P_{\lambda}(X,q,t)\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}
forms a linear basis for
\Lambda_{X}
. More generally, for each specialization
\rho
,
\{P_{\lambda}(\rho,q,t)\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}
can also be obtained from
p_{n}(\rho)
, as explained above.

Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, define

\displaystyle p_{\lambda}(\rho)=\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}p_{\lambda_{i}}(\rho).

Then
\{p_{\lambda}(X)\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}
is a linear basis for
\Lambda_{X}
.

For each fixed pair of parameters
q,t\in(0,1)
and
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
define the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\Lambda_{X}\times\Lambda_{X}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
as a bilinear map such that:

(A.5)

\displaystyle\langle p_{\lambda},p_{\mu}\rangle=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}}}{1-t^{\lambda_{i}}}\right]\left[\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}j^{m_{j}(\lambda)}(m_{j}(\lambda))!\right]

where
\delta_{\lambda\mu}=1
if and only if
\lambda=\mu
, and
m_{j}(\lambda)
is the number of parts in
\lambda
equal to
j
. Note that the scalar product defined above depends on parameters
(q,t)
. However, when
q=t
(
A.5
) does not depend on
q
or
t
.

Define
Q_{\lambda}(X;q,t)
to be a multiple of
P_{\lambda}(X,q,t)
such that

\displaystyle\langle P_{\lambda}(X,q,t),Q_{\lambda}(X,q,t)\rangle=1

For
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, define the skew MacDonald symmetric functions by

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)P_{\mu}(Y;q,t);

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda}(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)Q_{\mu}(Y;q,t);

It is known that (see Remarks 1. on Page 346 of
[
33
]
) for a single variable
x

\displaystyle P_{\lambda/\mu}(x)=\delta_{\lambda\prec\mu}\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|};\qquad Q_{\lambda/\mu}(x)=\delta_{\mu\prec\lambda}\phi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}

where
\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)
and
\phi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)
are independent of
x
and furthermore

\displaystyle\left.\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\right|_{q=t}=\left.\phi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\right|_{q=t}=1

Let
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, we write
\lambda\subseteq\mu
if

•

\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i}
, for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
X
be a countable set of variables, then

\displaystyle P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)=Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q.t)=0

unless
\mu\subseteq\lambda
. When
\mu\subseteq\lambda
,
Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q.t)
and
P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)
are homogeneous in
X
of degree
|\lambda|-|\mu|
.

Proof.

See (7.7) on Page 344 of
[
33
]
.
∎

In particular, when
q=t
,

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X;t,t)=Q_{\lambda}(X;t,t)=s_{\lambda}(X);

(A.6)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;t,t)=Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;t,t)=s_{\lambda/\mu}(X).

See (4.14) on Page 324 of
[
33
]
.

Definition A.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
q,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be parameters. Let
D_{-k,X}
be an operator acting on symmetric functions
\Lambda_{X}
.
For any analytic symmetric function
F(X)
satisfying

\displaystyle F(X)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}c_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t),

where
c_{\lambda}
’s are complex coefficients, define
D_{-k,X}F\in\Lambda_{X}
to be

(A.7)

\displaystyle D_{-k,X;q,t}F(X)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}c_{\lambda}\left\{(1-t^{-k})\left[\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}(q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{-i+1})^{k}\right]+t^{-kl(\lambda)}\right\}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t).

Let
W=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})
be an ordered set of variables. Define

(A.8)

\displaystyle D(W;q,t)=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(2\pi\mathbf{i})^{k}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{w_{k}t^{k-i}}{w_{i}q^{k-i}}}{\left(1-\frac{tw_{2}}{qw_{1}}\right)\ldots\left(1-\frac{tw_{k}}{qw_{k-1}}\right)}\prod_{i<j}\frac{(1-\frac{w_{i}}{w_{j}})(1-\frac{qw_{i}}{tw_{j}})}{\left(1-\frac{w_{i}}{tw_{j}}\right)\left(1-\frac{qw_{i}}{w_{j}}\right)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{dw_{i}}{w_{i}}

Define

(A.9)

\displaystyle L(W,X;q,t)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{x_{j}\in X}\frac{w_{i}-\frac{qx_{j}}{t}}{w_{i}-qx_{j}}

The following proposition is a slightly more general form of Proposition 4.10 of
[
17
]
.

Proposition A.3
.

Assume one of the following two conditions holds

(1)

q\in(0,1)
and
t\in(0,1)
; or

(2)

q\in(1,\infty)
and
t\in(1,\infty)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function analytic in a neighborhood of 0, and
f(0)\neq 0
. Let
g:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function analytic in a neighborhood of 0, and

\displaystyle g(z)=\frac{f(z)}{f(q^{-1}z)};

for
z
in a small neighborhood of
0
. Then

(A.10)

\displaystyle D_{-k,X;q,t}\left(\prod_{x_{i}\in X}f(x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{x_{i}\in X}f(x_{i})\right)\oint\cdots\oint D(W;q,t)L(W,X;q,t)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}g(w_{i})\right)

where the contours of the integral satisfy the following conditions

•

all the contours are in the neighborhood of
0
such that both
f
and
g
are analytic;

•

each contour enclose
0
and
\{qx_{i}\}_{x_{i}\in X}
;

•

If case (1) holds,
|w_{i}|\leq|tw_{i+1}|
for all
i\in[k-1]
;

•

If case (2) holds,
|w_{i}|\leq\left|\frac{1}{q}w_{i+1}\right|
for all
i\in[k-1]
;

L(W,X;q,t)
is given by (
A.9
), and
D(W;q,t)
is given by (
A.8
).

Proof.

See Proposition A.2 of
[
32
]
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
(a,q)_{\infty}=\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}(1-aq^{r})
and

(A.11)

\displaystyle\Pi(X,Y;q,t):=\frac{(tx_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}},\quad\Pi^{\prime}(X,Y):=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}).

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)Q_{\lambda}(Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(X;q,t)Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}(Y;q,t)=\Pi(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(Y;t,q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}Q_{\lambda}(X;q,t)Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}(Y;t,q)=\Pi^{\prime}(X,Y)

In particular, when
q=t
we obtain the Cauchy identities for Schur polynomials.

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}s_{\lambda}(X)s_{\lambda}(Y)=\prod_{i,j}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}};

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}s_{\lambda}(X)s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(Y)=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}).

Proof.

See (2.5), (4.13), and (5.4) in Section VI of
[
33
]
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Let
\Pi
,
\Pi^{\prime}
, and
L
be as in (
A.11
) and (
A.9
), then

\displaystyle\Pi(X,Y;q,t)=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{1}{n}p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y)\right),

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}(X,Y)=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n}p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y)\right),

\displaystyle L(X,Y;q,t)=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{-n}}{n}p_{n}(qX^{-1})p_{n}(Y)\right).

Proof.

The first identity follows from Page 310 of
[
33
]
. The other two follow from

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi(-X,Y;0,0)\right]^{-1},

\displaystyle L(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\Pi(qX^{-1},Y;0,t^{-1}).

∎

Lemma A.6
.

(Lemma 5.7 of
[
2
]
) Let
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, and
\xi>0
. Define

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon,\theta,\xi}:=\{w\in\mathbb{C}:\mathrm{dist}(w,[1,\infty))\leq\xi\}\cap\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|\arg(w-(1-\epsilon))|\leq\theta\}.

Let
\alpha>0
and suppose
N(\epsilon)\in{\mathbb{Z}}>0
such that
\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\epsilon N(\epsilon)>0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. Then for any fixed
\theta\in(0,\pi),\xi>0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(z;e^{-\epsilon})_{N(\epsilon)}}{(e^{-\epsilon\alpha}z;e^{-\epsilon})_{N(\epsilon)}}=\left(\frac{1-z}{1-e^{-\epsilon N(\epsilon)}z}\right)^{\alpha}\exp\left(O\left(\frac{\epsilon\min\{|z|,|z|^{2}\}}{|1-z|}\right)\right)

uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus R_{\epsilon,\theta,\xi}
and
\epsilon
arbitrarily small.

Lemma A.7
.

(Corollary A.2 in
[
17
]
) Let
d,h,k
be positive integers. Let
f
,
g_{1}
,…,
g_{d}
be meromorphic functions with possible poles at
{z_{1},...,z_{h}}
. Then for
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathbf{i})^{k}}\oint\ldots\oint\frac{1}{(v_{2}-v_{1})\cdots\ldots\cdot(v_{k}-v_{k-1})}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}g_{j}(v_{i})\right)\prod_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})dv_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{d-1}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint f(v)^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d}g_{j}(v)dv.

where the contours contain
\{z_{1},...,z_{h}\}
and on the left side we require that the
v_{i}
-contour is contained in the
v_{j}
-contour whenever
i<j
.

Lemma A.8
.

(Hurwitz’s theorem)Let
\{f_{k}\}
be a sequence of holomorphic functions on a connected open set
G
that converge uniformly on compact subsets of G to a holomorphic function
f
which is not constantly zero on G. If f has a zero of order
m
at
z_{0}
then for every small enough
\rho>0
and for sufficiently large
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending on
\rho
),
f_{k}
has precisely
m
zeroes in the disk defined by
|z-z_{0}|<\rho
, including multiplicity. Furthermore, these zeroes converge to
z_{0}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

See Page 178 of
[
1
]
, proof of Theorem 2.
∎

) gives
